================================================================================
  STAGE 12: MASTER'S / FIRST-YEAR GRADUATE — QUESTIONS AND ANSWERS
  PhD coursework and qualifying exams
================================================================================

How to use this file (note for the parent / teacher)
----------------------------------------------------
- There are 30 topics (12.1 to 12.30, the same as in topic.txt), in two
  groups: the Core graduate sequence (12.1-12.13: real, functional and
  complex analysis, several complex variables, graduate algebra,
  homological algebra, sheaves, commutative algebra, algebraic topology,
  differential geometry, Lie theory, measure-theoretic probability and
  PDE) and the Specialisation foundations (12.14-12.30: algebraic
  geometry, algebraic and analytic number theory, representation theory,
  harmonic analysis, stochastic calculus, ergodic theory, logic, category
  theory, combinatorics, numerical analysis, optimisation, statistics,
  mathematical physics, mathematical finance, inverse problems and
  algorithms).
  Each topic has:
    * WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN   the checklist of definitions, theorems,
                                  standard examples, counterexamples and
                                  methods in that topic
    * PART A — DESCRIPTIVE        12 questions (D1-D12): proofs,
                                  explanations, derivations and worked
                                  numerical problems, each with the answer
                                  and "Teacher explains" (the method step
                                  by step as a teacher shows it on the
                                  board, common mistakes, and a "Now you
                                  try" question)
    * PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY  12 questions (M1-M12): pure drills with
                                  answers. Most have many parts (a, b,
                                  c ...) so that EVERY case of the topic is
                                  practised again and again, from the
                                  easiest to the hardest — not just one
                                  example of each idea.
    * TEACHING TIPS               what to do if the student struggles, and
                                  how to make it harder
- In total: 360 descriptive + 360 mathematical questions.
- Level: first-year graduate / qualifying-exam mathematics. The core
  sequence (12.1-12.13) is what a typical PhD qualifying exam covers; the
  specialisation foundations are chosen by research area.
- What is new in Stage 12: SYNTHESIS AND ABSTRACTION. Theorems of Stage 11
  become tools; the student learns to recognise the same structure
  (a functor, a spectral decomposition, a cohomology theory, a
  martingale, a variational principle) in many guises, and to solve
  qualifying-exam problems quickly and completely.
- Conventions: as in A11.txt. N = {1, 2, 3, ...}; log = ln unless a base is
  written; m or λ is Lebesgue measure; a.e. = almost everywhere; ≅ means
  isomorphic; □ ends a proof; exact answers first, decimals with ≈.
- Method: for every theorem know its hypotheses and a counterexample when
  one is dropped; for every computation know which theorem justifies it.
  Use Python/SageMath/Macaulay2/R to CHECK calculations; write every
  solution in LaTeX. Practise past qualifying exams under time limits.
- Do 3 hours a day: 2 descriptive questions (full written proof) and
  2-3 drills per session, plus a weekly timed mixed paper.
- Revise Stage 11 (A11.txt) whenever the student finds something
  difficult: 11.1-11.3 before 12.1, 12.2, 12.12, 12.13, 12.18; 11.4-11.5
  before 12.15-12.16; 11.6-11.7, 11.17 before 12.9-12.11; 11.8-11.9 before
  12.5-12.8, 12.14, 12.17; 11.12-11.13 before 12.12, 12.19, 12.20;
  11.14-11.16 before 12.23-12.30.

================================================================================
TOPIC 12.1 — REAL ANALYSIS (GRADUATE): ABSTRACT MEASURE THEORY,
RADON-NIKODYM, DIFFERENTIATION OF MEASURES, RIESZ REPRESENTATION THEOREM
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - OUTER MEASURE on X: μ* : 𝒫(X) → [0, ∞] with μ*(∅) = 0, monotone and
    COUNTABLY SUBADDITIVE. CARATHÉODORY: E is μ*-measurable iff μ*(A) =
    μ*(A ∩ E) + μ*(A ∖ E) for every A ⊆ X (only "≥" needs checking). The
    measurable sets form a σ-algebra 𝓜 and μ*|𝓜 is a COMPLETE measure
    (every subset of a null set is measurable).
  - ALGEBRAS AND PREMEASURES: a premeasure ρ on an algebra 𝒜 is countably
    additive whenever the union happens to lie in 𝒜. CARATHÉODORY-HAHN
    EXTENSION: μ*(E) = inf{∑ρ(Aₙ) : E ⊆ ⋃Aₙ, Aₙ ∈ 𝒜} is an outer measure
    whose restriction to σ(𝒜) extends ρ; the extension is UNIQUE if ρ is
    σ-finite. Example: F increasing right-continuous gives μ_F(a, b] =
    F(b) − F(a) (Lebesgue-Stieltjes measures).
  - DYNKIN'S π-λ THEOREM: a π-system (closed under finite intersections)
    𝒫 lies in a λ-system 𝓛 (contains X, closed under complements/proper
    differences and countable DISJOINT unions) ⇒ σ(𝒫) ⊆ 𝓛. Consequence:
    two measures agreeing on a generating π-system and σ-finite on it
    (or finite with equal total mass) are equal. Fails otherwise: μ =
    #(· ∩ Q), ν = 2#(· ∩ Q) agree on all intervals (a, b].
  - COMPLETION: 𝓜̄ = {E ∪ N : E ∈ 𝓜, N ⊆ null set}. Lebesgue ℒ is the
    completion of Borel 𝔅; #𝔅 = 𝔠 < 2^𝔠 = #ℒ, so ℒ ≠ 𝔅.
  - RADON MEASURES on a locally compact Hausdorff (LCH) space: Borel,
    finite on compacts, outer regular on Borel sets, inner regular (by
    compacts) on open sets. On Rⁿ (or any σ-compact metric space) EVERY
    Borel measure finite on compacts is Radon and regular. LUSIN: a
    measurable f equals a continuous function off a set of small measure;
    C_c is dense in Lᵖ(μ), 1 ≤ p < ∞.
  - SIGNED AND COMPLEX MEASURES: ν countably additive with values in
    (−∞, ∞] (or C). HAHN: X = P ⊔ N with P positive, N negative (unique up
    to |ν|-null sets). JORDAN: ν = ν⁺ − ν⁻ with ν⁺ ⊥ ν⁻, ν⁺(E) = ν(E ∩ P),
    minimal among all such splittings. TOTAL VARIATION |ν| = ν⁺ + ν⁻,
    |ν|(E) = sup ∑|ν(Eⱼ)| over partitions; if dν = f dμ then d|ν| = |f|dμ.
    POLAR FORM dν = h d|ν| with |h| = 1.
  - ABSOLUTE CONTINUITY ν ≪ μ: μ(E) = 0 ⇒ ν(E) = 0. For FINITE ν this is
    equivalent to the ε-δ condition μ(E) < δ ⇒ |ν|(E) < ε (false for
    infinite ν: dν = dx/x on (0, 1)). MUTUAL SINGULARITY ν ⊥ μ: they live
    on disjoint sets (δ₀ ⊥ m, Cantor measure ⊥ m).
  - RADON-NIKODYM: μ σ-finite, ν σ-finite signed (or complex) with ν ≪ μ
    ⇒ there is a unique (μ-a.e.) f with ν(E) = ∫_E f dμ; f = dν/dμ.
    CHAIN RULE dλ/dμ = (dλ/dν)(dν/dμ); dμ/dν = 1/(dν/dμ) if μ ~ ν.
    FAILS without σ-finiteness: m ≪ counting measure on [0, 1], no density.
    Proof by von Neumann's L² trick or by Hahn decompositions.
  - LEBESGUE DECOMPOSITION: ν = ν_ac + ν_s uniquely, ν_ac ≪ μ, ν_s ⊥ μ.
    On R relative to m: ν = ac + pure point (atoms) + SINGULAR CONTINUOUS
    (e.g. Cantor measure, or ∑εₙ2⁻ⁿ with P(εₙ = 1) = p ≠ ½).
  - APPLICATIONS OF R-N: (Lᵖ)* = L^q (1/p + 1/q = 1) for 1 < p < ∞, and for
    p = 1 when μ is σ-finite; (L^∞)* ⊋ L¹. Conditional expectation E[f|𝒢]
    = d(f μ)|𝒢 / dμ|𝒢. Densities of distributions, pushforward measures.
  - VITALI COVERING LEMMA: from finitely many balls pick disjoint Bⱼ with
    ⋃B ⊆ ⋃3Bⱼ. HARDY-LITTLEWOOD MAXIMAL FUNCTION Mf(x) = sup_r (1/|B(x,
    r)|)∫_{B(x,r)}|f|: WEAK (1,1) m{Mf > t} ≤ (3ⁿ/t)‖f‖₁; strong (p, p) for
    1 < p ≤ ∞; Mf ∉ L¹(Rⁿ) unless f = 0 a.e.
  - LEBESGUE DIFFERENTIATION THEOREM: f ∈ L¹_loc(Rⁿ) ⇒ for a.e. x,
    (1/|B(x, r)|)∫_{B(x,r)}|f(y) − f(x)| dy → 0 (x is a LEBESGUE POINT);
    also for families "shrinking nicely". DENSITY: for measurable E, the
    density lim m(E ∩ B(x, r))/m(B(x, r)) is 1 a.e. on E, 0 a.e. off E.
  - DIFFERENTIATION OF MEASURES: for a Radon ν on Rⁿ with dν = f dm + ν_s,
    ν(B(x, r))/m(B(x, r)) → f(x) m-a.e.; the singular part has derivative
    0 m-a.e. (and ∞ at ν_s-a.e. point, e.g. at atoms).
  - BOUNDED VARIATION: T_F[a, b] = sup ∑|F(xⱼ) − F(xⱼ₋₁)|; F ∈ BV ⇔ F =
    difference of increasing functions (Jordan). LEBESGUE: monotone (hence
    BV) functions are differentiable a.e., ∫ₐᵇ F′ ≤ F(b) − F(a).
  - ABSOLUTE CONTINUITY OF FUNCTIONS: ∀ε ∃δ: ∑(bⱼ − aⱼ) < δ ⇒ ∑|F(bⱼ) −
    F(aⱼ)| < ε. FTC FOR LEBESGUE: F is AC on [a, b] ⇔ F(x) = F(a) + ∫ₐˣ
    F′ with F′ ∈ L¹; then T_F = ∫|F′|. Lipschitz ⇒ AC ⇒ BV ∩ C; the
    CANTOR FUNCTION is continuous BV with F′ = 0 a.e. but not AC. For
    increasing right-continuous F: F AC ⇔ μ_F ≪ m, and dμ_F/dm = F′.
  - RIESZ REPRESENTATION (RIESZ-MARKOV-KAKUTANI): X LCH, Λ a POSITIVE
    linear functional on C_c(X) ⇒ there is a unique Radon μ with Λf = ∫f
    dμ; μ(U) = sup{Λf : 0 ≤ f ≤ 1, supp f ⊆ U} for open U. DUAL FORM:
    C₀(X)* ≅ M(X), the regular complex Borel measures, with ‖Λ‖ = |μ|(X).
    Examples: point evaluation ↔ δₓ, Riemann integral ↔ m, Stieltjes
    integral ↔ μ_F.
  - WEAK-* (VAGUE) CONVERGENCE: μₙ → μ iff ∫f dμₙ → ∫f dμ for all f ∈
    C₀(X). Bounded sets of M(X) are weak-* sequentially compact (X
    separable metric; Banach-Alaoglu, Helly selection). Mass can escape
    (δₙ → 0); with tightness the limit of probability measures is a
    probability measure (narrow convergence).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Let μ* be an outer measure on X. (a) Prove that the μ*-measurable
    sets form a σ-algebra 𝓜 and that μ*|𝓜 is a complete measure. (b) On
    X = {1, 2, 3} let μ*(A) = [A ∩ {1, 2} ≠ ∅] + [3 ∈ A] ([·] = 1 if true,
    0 if false). Find 𝓜 and μ on 𝓜.
    Answer: (a) proof below. (b) 𝓜 = {∅, {3}, {1, 2}, X} with measures
    0, 1, 1, 2.
    Teacher explains: (a) The condition μ*(A) = μ*(A ∩ E) + μ*(A ∩ Eᶜ) is
    symmetric in E and Eᶜ, so 𝓜 is closed under complements, and "≤" is
    automatic by subadditivity. FINITE UNIONS: for E, F ∈ 𝓜 and any A,
       μ*(A) = μ*(A ∩ E) + μ*(A ∩ Eᶜ ∩ F) + μ*(A ∩ Eᶜ ∩ Fᶜ)
             ≥ μ*(A ∩ (E ∪ F)) + μ*(A ∩ (E ∪ F)ᶜ),
    since A ∩ (E ∪ F) = (A ∩ E) ∪ (A ∩ Eᶜ ∩ F). COUNTABLE DISJOINT UNIONS:
    let Eⱼ ∈ 𝓜 be disjoint, Bₙ = E₁ ∪ ... ∪ Eₙ, B = ⋃Eⱼ. Testing Eₙ on A ∩
    Bₙ gives μ*(A ∩ Bₙ) = μ*(A ∩ Eₙ) + μ*(A ∩ Bₙ₋₁), so by induction
    μ*(A ∩ Bₙ) = ∑_{j≤n} μ*(A ∩ Eⱼ). Since Bₙ ∈ 𝓜 and Bᶜ ⊆ Bₙᶜ,
       μ*(A) ≥ ∑_{j≤n} μ*(A ∩ Eⱼ) + μ*(A ∩ Bᶜ).
    Let n → ∞ and use countable subadditivity:
       μ*(A) ≥ ∑_j μ*(A ∩ Eⱼ) + μ*(A ∩ Bᶜ) ≥ μ*(A ∩ B) + μ*(A ∩ Bᶜ).
    So B ∈ 𝓜 (an algebra closed under countable disjoint unions is a
    σ-algebra), and A = B turns the first inequality into countable
    additivity. COMPLETENESS: if μ*(N) = 0 then μ*(A ∩ N) + μ*(A ∖ N) ≤ 0 +
    μ*(A). (b) μ* is the sum of two outer measures, each "is the set
    touched?". Test {3}: μ*(A ∩ {3}) + μ*(A ∖ {3}) = [3 ∈ A] + [A meets
    {1, 2}] = μ*(A) for all A, so {3} ∈ 𝓜 and hence {1, 2} ∈ 𝓜. Test {1}
    with A = {1, 2}: μ*({1}) + μ*({2}) = 2 ≠ 1 = μ*({1, 2}); so {1} ∉ 𝓜,
    and likewise {2}, {1, 3}, {2, 3}. Common mistakes: checking the
    splitting condition only for A = X (it must hold for EVERY test set
    A); forgetting that μ* may be defined on all subsets while only 𝓜
    carries a measure. Now you try: on an uncountable X, μ*(A) = 0 for A
    countable, 1 otherwise. Find 𝓜. (countable and co-countable sets)

D2. (a) State the Carathéodory-Hahn extension theorem. (b) Prove, using
    the π-λ theorem, that two measures on σ(𝒫) that agree on a π-system
    𝒫 and are finite on sets Pₙ ∈ 𝒫 with Pₙ ↑ X are equal. (c) Show that
    uniqueness fails without the finiteness hypothesis.
    Answer: (a) a premeasure on an algebra extends to σ(𝒜), uniquely if
    σ-finite. (b) The "good sets" form a λ-system. (c) #(· ∩ Q) and
    2#(· ∩ Q) on 𝔅(R).
    Teacher explains: (a) If ρ is a premeasure on an algebra 𝒜 (ρ(∅) = 0,
    ρ(⊔Aₙ) = ∑ρ(Aₙ) whenever ⊔Aₙ ∈ 𝒜), then μ*(E) = inf{∑ρ(Aₙ) : E ⊆ ⋃Aₙ}
    is an outer measure, every set of 𝒜 is μ*-measurable, μ* = ρ on 𝒜,
    so μ = μ*|σ(𝒜) extends ρ. If ρ is σ-finite the extension is unique.
    (b) Fix P ∈ 𝒫 with μ(P) = ν(P) < ∞ and let 𝓛 = {A ∈ σ(𝒫) : μ(A ∩ P)
    = ν(A ∩ P)}. Then X ∈ 𝓛; if A ⊆ B are in 𝓛 then μ((B ∖ A) ∩ P) =
    μ(B ∩ P) − μ(A ∩ P) (legal: finite numbers) = ν((B ∖ A) ∩ P); and 𝓛 is
    closed under increasing unions by continuity from below. So 𝓛 is a
    λ-system; it contains the π-system 𝒫 (as 𝒫 ∩ P ⊆ 𝒫). By Dynkin,
    σ(𝒫) ⊆ 𝓛. Now for any A: μ(A ∩ Pₙ) = ν(A ∩ Pₙ) and let n → ∞. The
    finiteness is used exactly in the subtraction step. (c) Let μ(A) =
    #(A ∩ Q) and ν(A) = 2#(A ∩ Q). The half-open intervals (a, b] (plus
    ∅) form a π-system generating 𝔅(R), and every non-empty (a, b]
    contains infinitely many rationals, so μ = ν = ∞ there. But μ({0}) =
    1 ≠ 2 = ν({0}). No sequence Pₙ of finite measure exists. Common
    mistakes: forgetting that 𝒫 must be closed under intersection (see
    M2 e); applying the result to finite measures with different total
    mass. Now you try: two probability measures on R with the same
    distribution function F(a) = μ(−∞, a] — are they equal? (yes: the
    rays form a π-system and the total masses are both 1)

D3. (a) Show that the Lebesgue σ-algebra ℒ is strictly larger than the
    Borel σ-algebra 𝔅. (b) Let μ be a finite Borel measure on a metric
    space X. Prove that for every Borel E and ε > 0 there are F closed and
    U open with F ⊆ E ⊆ U and μ(U ∖ F) < ε. (c) For μ = δ₀ + m|[0,1] find
    μ(Q ∩ [0, 1]) and an open U ⊇ Q ∩ [0, 1] with μ(U) < 1.01.
    Answer: (a) cardinality 𝔠 versus 2^𝔠. (b) the "good sets" form a
    σ-algebra containing the closed sets. (c) 1; U = (−δ, δ) ∪ ⋃ small
    intervals around the other rationals of total length < 0.01.
    Teacher explains: (a) 𝔅 is generated by the countable family of
    rational intervals; building σ-algebras by transfinite recursion
    over ω₁ stages shows #𝔅 = 𝔠. The Cantor set C has m(C) = 0 and #C =
    𝔠, and ℒ is complete, so EVERY subset of C is in ℒ: #ℒ ≥ 2^𝔠 > 𝔠.
    Hence some null set is not Borel (ℒ is the completion of 𝔅). (b) Call
    E good if the statement holds. If E is good so is Eᶜ (swap F ↔ Uᶜ).
    If Eₖ are good with Fₖ ⊆ Eₖ ⊆ Uₖ, μ(Uₖ ∖ Fₖ) < ε2⁻ᵏ, put U = ⋃Uₖ and
    choose N with μ(⋃Fₖ ∖ ⋃_{k≤N}Fₖ) < ε (continuity, μ finite); F =
    ⋃_{k≤N}Fₖ is closed and μ(U ∖ F) < 2ε. So good sets form a σ-algebra.
    Every closed F is good: Uₙ = {x : d(x, F) < 1/n} is open and Uₙ ↓ F,
    so μ(Uₙ ∖ F) → 0. Closed sets generate 𝔅: all Borel sets are good.
    On Rⁿ closed sets are countable unions of compacts, so μ is Radon.
    (c) 0 ∈ Q, so μ(Q ∩ [0, 1]) = δ₀({0}) + m(Q ∩ [0,1]) = 1 + 0 = 1.
    Enumerate the other rationals q₁, q₂, ... of [0, 1] and let U = (−δ,
    δ) ∪ ⋃(qₖ − 0.0025·2⁻ᵏ, qₖ + 0.0025·2⁻ᵏ) with δ = 0.0025: μ(U) ≤ 1 +
    δ + 0.005 = 1.0075 < 1.01 (only (0, δ) of (−δ, δ) lies in [0, 1]).
    Common mistakes: thinking regularity needs local compactness (for
    finite measures, metric suffices); forgetting the atom at 0.
    Now you try: is every σ-finite Borel measure on R regular?
    (no: counting measure on Q is σ-finite but μ(U) = ∞ for every open
    U ≠ ∅, while μ({0}) = 1; it is not finite on compacts)

D4. (a) Prove the Hahn decomposition theorem for a finite signed measure
    ν. (b) For ν(A) = ∫_{A∩[−2,2]} (x² − 1) dx + δ₀(A) find a Hahn
    decomposition, ν⁺, ν⁻, |ν| and ν(R).
    Answer: (b) P = {1 ≤ |x| ≤ 2} ∪ {0}; ν⁺(R) = 11/3, ν⁻(R) = 4/3,
    |ν|(R) = 5, ν(R) = 7/3.
    Teacher explains: (a) LEMMA: if ν(E) > 0 then E contains a POSITIVE
    set A with ν(A) ≥ ν(E). (Remove greedily: let n₁ be the least n such
    that E has a subset of measure ≤ −1/n₁, remove such a subset, and
    repeat; what remains is positive, and removing negative pieces only
    increased ν.) Now let s = sup{ν(P) : P positive} ≤ ν(X)⁺ < ∞, pick
    positive Pₙ with ν(Pₙ) → s; P = ⋃Pₙ is positive (a countable union of
    positive sets is positive) and ν(P) = s. N = Pᶜ is negative: if E ⊆ N
    had ν(E) > 0, the lemma gives a positive A ⊆ E with ν(A) > 0, and P ∪
    A is positive with ν(P ∪ A) > s, a contradiction. Then ν⁺(E) = ν(E ∩
    P), ν⁻(E) = −ν(E ∩ N) is the JORDAN decomposition. (b) The density
    x² − 1 is ≥ 0 exactly where |x| ≥ 1, and the atom at 0 is positive,
    so P = [−2, −1] ∪ [1, 2] ∪ {0} and N = (−1, 0) ∪ (0, 1) (up to null
    sets, endpoints may go either way). Then
       ν⁺(R) = 2∫₁² (x² − 1) dx + 1 = 2(7/3 − 1) + 1 = 8/3 + 1 = 11/3,
       ν⁻(R) = ∫₋₁¹ (1 − x²) dx = 2 − 2/3 = 4/3,
    |ν|(R) = 11/3 + 4/3 = 5 and ν(R) = 11/3 − 4/3 = 7/3. Check: ∫₋₂²(x² −
    1) dx = 16/3 − 4 = 4/3, plus 1 is 7/3 ✓. Common mistakes: "P = {x :
    density > 0}" ignoring atoms; thinking the Hahn decomposition is
    unique (it is unique only up to |ν|-null sets); writing |ν| = |ν(·)|
    (|ν|(R) = 5, but |ν(R)| = 7/3). Now you try: ν = m|[0,2] − m|[1,3].
    Find ν⁺ and ν⁻. (ν⁺ = m|[0,1], ν⁻ = m|[2,3]: the overlap cancels)

D5. (a) Let ν be a FINITE measure and μ a measure on the same σ-algebra.
    Prove that ν ≪ μ iff for every ε > 0 there is δ > 0 with μ(E) < δ ⇒
    ν(E) < ε. (b) Show that "⇒" fails for dν = dx/x on (0, 1), μ = m.
    (c) Give the Lebesgue decomposition of ν = δ_{1/2} + 3m|[0,1] + μ_c
    (μ_c the Cantor measure) with respect to m.
    Answer: (a) ⇐ is immediate; ⇒ by Borel-Cantelli. (b) ν ≪ m but
    ν(0, δ) = ∞ for every δ. (c) ν_ac = 3m|[0,1], ν_s = δ_{1/2} + μ_c.
    Teacher explains: (a) ⇐: if μ(E) = 0 then μ(E) < δ for every δ, so
    ν(E) < ε for every ε, i.e. ν(E) = 0. ⇒ by contradiction: suppose some
    ε > 0 fails for every δ; choose Eₙ with μ(Eₙ) < 2⁻ⁿ but ν(Eₙ) ≥ ε.
    Let F = lim sup Eₙ = ⋂_k ⋃_{n≥k} Eₙ. Then μ(F) ≤ ∑_{n≥k} 2⁻ⁿ = 2¹⁻ᵏ for
    all k, so μ(F) = 0. But ν(⋃_{n≥k} Eₙ) ≥ ε for every k and these sets
    DECREASE, so continuity from above (ν FINITE!) gives ν(F) ≥ ε > 0,
    contradicting ν ≪ μ. (b) ν(E) = ∫_E dx/x is zero whenever m(E) = 0,
    so ν ≪ m, yet m((0, δ)) = δ and ν((0, δ)) = ∫₀^δ dx/x = ∞. The proof
    of (a) breaks exactly at continuity from above. (c) The density part
    3𝟙_[0,1] dx is ≪ m. The atom δ_{1/2} lives on {1/2} (m-null); μ_c
    lives on the Cantor set C (m-null). Both are ⊥ m, and so is their sum
    (it lives on C ∪ {1/2}). Hence ν_ac = 3m|[0,1] and ν_s = δ_{1/2} + μ_c;
    further ν_s = pure point δ_{1/2} + singular continuous μ_c. Total
    masses: ν(R) = 1 + 3 + 1 = 5. Common mistakes: applying the ε-δ
    criterion to infinite ν; thinking μ_c ≪ m because μ_c has no atoms
    (continuous ≠ absolutely continuous). Now you try: for f ∈ L¹(μ), show
    ν(E) = ∫_E|f| dμ satisfies the ε-δ condition. (ν is finite and ν ≪ μ;
    or truncate f as in 11.1 D12)

D6. State the Radon-Nikodym theorem and prove it for finite measures μ,
    ν ≥ 0 by von Neumann's Hilbert-space method, obtaining the Lebesgue
    decomposition at the same time.
    Answer: if μ, ν are σ-finite and ν ≪ μ, then ν = f dμ with f ≥ 0
    unique μ-a.e.; proof: represent f ↦ ∫f dν on L²(μ + ν).
    Teacher explains: Let ρ = μ + ν (finite). The map T(φ) = ∫φ dν is
    well defined and bounded on L²(ρ):
       |∫φ dν| ≤ ∫|φ| dρ ≤ ρ(X)^(1/2) ‖φ‖_{L²(ρ)}   (Cauchy-Schwarz).
    By the Riesz representation of Hilbert-space functionals there is g ∈
    L²(ρ) with ∫φ dν = ∫φ g dρ, i.e.
       ∫φ(1 − g) dν = ∫φ g dμ   for all φ ∈ L²(ρ).          (∗)
    Taking φ = 𝟙_E gives ν(E) = ∫_E g dρ, so 0 ≤ g ≤ 1 ρ-a.e. (test E =
    {g < 0} and E = {g > 1}). Let S = {g = 1}. With φ = 𝟙_S, (∗) gives
    0 = ∫_S g dμ = μ(S), so ν_s := ν|_S is ⊥ μ. Off S, put φ = (1 + g +
    ... + gⁿ)𝟙_{E∖S} in (∗):
       ∫_{E∖S} (1 − gⁿ⁺¹) dν = ∫_{E∖S} g(1 + g + ... + gⁿ) dμ.
    As n → ∞, gⁿ⁺¹ → 0 on Sᶜ and the right-hand integrand increases, so by
    MCT ν(E ∖ S) = ∫_E h dμ with h = g/(1 − g) on Sᶜ, h = 0 on S. Thus
    ν = h dμ + ν_s (LEBESGUE DECOMPOSITION). If ν ≪ μ then ν(S) = 0 (as
    μ(S) = 0), so ν = h dμ. UNIQUENESS: if ∫_E h dμ = ∫_E h′ dμ for all E,
    take E = {h > h′} ∩ {h ≤ n}. σ-FINITE case: split X into countably
    many pieces of finite μ and ν measure and glue the densities; signed
    ν: apply to ν⁺ and ν⁻. Common mistakes: forgetting that h may be
    infinite-valued / non-integrable when ν is only σ-finite; claiming the
    theorem for non-σ-finite μ (see D7). Now you try: if ν ≤ μ (ν(E) ≤
    μ(E) for all E), what can you say about dν/dμ? (0 ≤ dν/dμ ≤ 1 a.e.)

D7. Compute the Radon-Nikodym derivatives, and explain the failure in
    (e): (a) dν/dm on [0, 2] for ν(A) = ∫_A 3x² dx; (b) dm/dμ on [0, ∞)
    for dμ = e⁻ˣ dx; (c) dν/dμ for μ = δ₀ + δ₁ + 2δ₂, ν = 3δ₀ + δ₂;
    (d) the density of the pushforward T₊m of m on (0, 1) under T(x) = x²;
    (e) m ≪ c, c = counting measure on [0, 1]: is there dm/dc?
    Answer: (a) 3x²; (b) eˣ; (c) 3 at 0, 0 at 1, 1/2 at 2; (d) 1/(2√y);
    (e) no.
    Teacher explains: The recipe: find h with ν(E) = ∫_E h dμ for all E.
    (a) Read it off: h = 3x². (b) μ and m are EQUIVALENT (each ≪ the
    other, e⁻ˣ > 0), and dm/dμ = 1/(dμ/dm) = 1/e⁻ˣ = eˣ. Check: ∫_E eˣ dμ =
    ∫_E eˣe⁻ˣ dx = m(E) ✓. (c) On atoms, h(x) = ν({x})/μ({x}): h(0) = 3/1,
    h(1) = 0/1 = 0, h(2) = 1/2. (Values off {0, 1, 2} are irrelevant: a
    μ-null set.) (d) T₊m(E) = m(T⁻¹E). For E = (0, t]: m{x : x² ≤ t} = √t,
    so the distribution function is √y and the density is d√y/dy =
    1/(2√y). General CHANGE-OF-VARIABLES rule: for T a C¹ bijection, the
    density of T₊(f dx) is f(T⁻¹y)|(T⁻¹)′(y)|. (e) c(E) = 0 only for E =
    ∅, so trivially m ≪ c. If m(E) = ∑_{x∈E} h(x) for all E, then E = {x}
    gives h(x) = m({x}) = 0 for every x, so m([0, 1]) = 0: contradiction.
    The hypothesis that fails is σ-FINITENESS of c on the uncountable
    [0, 1]. Common mistakes: dividing densities the wrong way round;
    forgetting the Jacobian factor |(T⁻¹)′| in (d). Now you try: the chain
    rule: dλ/dν = 2x and dν/dm = 3x² on (0, 1). Find dλ/dm. (6x³)

D8. Use the Radon-Nikodym theorem to identify the dual of Lᵖ. (a) Sketch
    the proof that every bounded linear Λ on Lᵖ(μ), 1 ≤ p < ∞, μ finite,
    has the form Λf = ∫fg dμ with g ∈ L^q and ‖Λ‖ = ‖g‖_q. (b) Compute
    ‖Λ‖ for Λf = ∫₀¹ x f(x) dx on L³[0, 1] and find an extremal f.
    (c) Explain why (L^∞[0, 1])* ≠ L¹[0, 1].
    Answer: (b) ‖Λ‖ = (2/5)^(2/3) ≈ 0.54288, attained at f = √x.
    (c) a Hahn-Banach extension of f ↦ f(0) from C[0, 1] is not given by
    any g ∈ L¹.
    Teacher explains: (a) Define ν(E) = Λ(𝟙_E). It is finitely additive by
    linearity; if Eₙ ↓ ∅ then ‖𝟙_{Eₙ}‖ₚ = μ(Eₙ)^(1/p) → 0 (here p < ∞ is
    ESSENTIAL), so ν(Eₙ) → 0: ν is a (signed or complex) measure. If μ(E)
    = 0 then 𝟙_E = 0 in Lᵖ, so ν(E) = 0: ν ≪ μ. Let g = dν/dμ. Then Λf =
    ∫fg for simple f by linearity. To show g ∈ L^q (1 < p), test Λ on
    fₙ = |g|^(q−1) sgn(ḡ)𝟙_{|g|≤n}: ∫_{|g|≤n}|g|^q ≤ ‖Λ‖(∫_{|g|≤n}|g|^q)^(1/p),
    so (∫_{|g|≤n}|g|^q)^(1/q) ≤ ‖Λ‖; let n → ∞ (MCT). (For p = 1 show |g| ≤
    ‖Λ‖ a.e.) Simple functions are dense in Lᵖ, so Λf = ∫fg everywhere,
    and Hölder gives ‖Λ‖ = ‖g‖_q. (b) p = 3, q = 3/2, g(x) = x:
       ‖Λ‖ = (∫₀¹ x^(3/2) dx)^(2/3) = (2/5)^(2/3) ≈ 0.54288.
    Equality in Hölder needs |f|ᵖ ∝ |g|^q, i.e. f = g^(q−1) = x^(1/2):
    Λ(√x) = ∫x^(3/2) = 2/5 and ‖√x‖₃ = (2/5)^(1/3), ratio (2/5)^(2/3) ✓.
    (c) ℓ(f) = f(0) is bounded on C[0, 1] ⊆ L^∞ with norm 1; extend it by
    Hahn-Banach to L^∞. If ℓ(f) = ∫fg with g ∈ L¹, take continuous fₙ
    with fₙ(0) = 1, 0 ≤ fₙ ≤ 1, supported in [0, 1/n]: ∫fₙg → 0 by DCT,
    but ℓ(fₙ) = 1. Common mistakes: forgetting where p < ∞ is used; for p
    = 1 dropping σ-finiteness (M12 q). Now you try: ‖Λ‖ for Λf = ∫₀¹ f
    on L²[0, 1]. (1: g = 1, ‖g‖₂ = 1)

D9. (a) Prove the finite Vitali covering lemma: from finitely many balls
    B₁, ..., B_N in Rⁿ one can pick disjoint B_{j₁}, ..., B_{j_k} with ⋃Bᵢ
    ⊆ ⋃3B_{j_l}. (b) Deduce the weak (1,1) inequality m{Mf > t} ≤
    (3ⁿ/t)‖f‖₁. (c) For f = 𝟙_[0,1] on R compute the centred maximal
    function Mf and m{Mf > t}, and show Mf ∉ L¹.
    Answer: (c) Mf = 1 on (0, 1), 1/2 at 0 and 1, 1/(2x) for x ≥ 1,
    1/(2(1 − x)) for x ≤ 0; m{Mf > t} = 1/t − 1 for 0 < t < 1/2, 1 for
    1/2 ≤ t < 1, 0 for t ≥ 1.
    Teacher explains: (a) GREEDY: take the largest ball B_{j₁}; discard
    all balls meeting it; from the rest take the largest, and so on. If a
    discarded B meets a chosen B′ of radius r′ ≥ r(B), then B ⊆ 3B′ (any
    point of B is within 2r + r′ ≤ 3r′ of the centre of B′). (b) Let K ⊆
    {Mf > t} be compact. Each x ∈ K has a ball Bₓ with ∫_{Bₓ}|f| > t m(Bₓ);
    finitely many cover K; apply (a):
       m(K) ≤ ∑ m(3B_{j_l}) = 3ⁿ∑ m(B_{j_l}) < (3ⁿ/t)∑∫_{B_{j_l}}|f|
            ≤ (3ⁿ/t)‖f‖₁ (the chosen balls are disjoint).
    Inner regularity of m (sup over K) gives the claim. (c) Mf(x) =
    sup_r m([x − r, x + r] ∩ [0, 1])/(2r). For 0 < x < 1, small r gives
    ratio 1, and ratio ≤ 1 always: Mf = 1. At x = 0 the overlap is r for r
    ≤ 1: ratio 1/2, and 1/(2r) after: Mf(0) = 1/2. For x > 1 and x − 1 ≤ r
    ≤ x the ratio is (r + 1 − x)/(2r) = 1/2 + (1 − x)/(2r), increasing in
    r; for r ≥ x it is 1/(2r), decreasing. The maximum is at r = x:
    Mf(x) = 1/(2x). By symmetry Mf(x) = 1/(2(1 − x)) for x < 0. For t <
    1/2: Mf > t on (1 − 1/(2t), 1/(2t)), which has length 1/t − 1. Since
    ∫₁^∞ dx/(2x) = ∞, Mf ∉ L¹ — typical: Mf(x) ≥ c/|x| for large |x|
    whenever f ≠ 0. The weak bound 3‖f‖₁/t = 3/t is indeed ≥ 1/t − 1.
    Common mistakes: confusing centred and uncentred maximal functions;
    expecting a strong (1,1) bound. Now you try: the uncentred maximal
    function (sup over all intervals containing x) at x = 3. (1/3, from
    the interval [0, 3])

D10. (a) State the Lebesgue differentiation theorem and sketch its proof
     from the maximal inequality. (b) Is 0 a Lebesgue point of f =
     𝟙_[0,∞)? of f(x) = sin(1/x), f(0) = 0? (c) Find the density at the
     origin of E = {(x, y) : |y| < x} ⊆ R² and of E′ = {(x, y) : y > x²}.
     Answer: (b) no; no (the averages of |f| tend to 2/π). (c) 1/4; 1/2.
     Teacher explains: (a) THEOREM: for f ∈ L¹_loc(Rⁿ), a.e. x satisfies
        lim_{r→0} (1/m(B(x, r))) ∫_{B(x,r)} |f(y) − f(x)| dy = 0.
     SKETCH: the claim is local, so let f ∈ L¹. Put Ωf(x) = lim sup of
     the averages of |f(y) − f(x)|. For continuous g, Ωg = 0. Given ε, pick
     g ∈ C_c with ‖f − g‖₁ < ε and write h = f − g. Then Ωf ≤ Ωh ≤ Mh +
     |h|, so
        m{Ωf > 2t} ≤ m{Mh > t} + m{|h| > t} ≤ (3ⁿ/t)ε + (1/t)ε.
     As ε is arbitrary, m{Ωf > 2t} = 0 for each t > 0; take t = 1/k. The
     same holds for families E_r shrinking nicely (E_r ⊆ B(x, r), m(E_r) ≥
     c m(B(x, r))), e.g. one-sided intervals: F(x) = ∫₀ˣ f has F′ = f a.e.
     (b) For 𝟙_[0,∞) the averages over [−r, r] equal 1/2 for every r, but
     (1/2r)∫|f − c| = (|1 − c| + |c|)/2 ≥ 1/2 for every c: 0 is not a
     Lebesgue point for any value (a jump). For sin(1/x): the average of f
     is 0 (f is odd), but (1/2r)∫_{−r}^{r}|sin(1/x)| dx → (1/2π)∫₀^{2π}|sin θ|
     dθ = 2/π ≠ 0, because near 0 the function runs through many full
     periods; similarly no value c works. (c) E is a sector of angle π/2
     (between the lines y = ±x, x > 0), so m(E ∩ B(0, r)) = (π/2)r²/2 and
     the ratio is (πr²/4)/(πr²) = 1/4. For E′: the parabola is tangent to
     the x-axis, so E′ ∩ B(0, r) differs from the upper half-disc by a set
     of area O(r³); the density is 1/2. Common mistakes: "average → f(x)"
     is weaker than the Lebesgue point condition (sin(1/x)); a density can
     fail to exist (M8). Now you try: the density at 0 of {|y| < x²}.
     (0: its area in B(0, r) is O(r³))

D11. (a) Compute the total variation of F(x) = x³ − 3x on [−2, 2] in two
     ways. (b) Show x sin(1/x) is not BV on [0, 1] while x² sin(1/x) is AC.
     (c) Show that the Cantor function c is continuous, of bounded
     variation, with c′ = 0 a.e., but is not AC, and relate this to μ_c.
     Answer: (a) 12. (b) partial sums ∑ 2/((k + ½)π) diverge; |F′| ≤ 3.
     (c) c(1) − c(0) = 1 ≠ 0 = ∫c′; μ_c ⊥ m.
     Teacher explains: (a) PIECEWISE MONOTONE: F′ = 3x² − 3 vanishes at
     ±1; F(−2) = −2, F(−1) = 2, F(1) = −2, F(2) = 2. On each monotone piece
     the variation is the change: T = 4 + 4 + 4 = 12. FTC FORM: F is C¹,
     hence AC, so T = ∫₋₂²|3x² − 3| dx = 2(∫₀¹(3 − 3x²) + ∫₁²(3x² − 3)) = 2(2
     + 4) = 12 ✓. JORDAN: F = P − N with P(x) = positive variation on
     [−2, x], both increasing. (b) At xₖ = 1/((k + ½)π), x sin(1/x) = ±xₖ
     with alternating signs, so the partition through these points gives
     ∑(xₖ + xₖ₊₁) ≥ ∑ 2/((k + 3/2)π) = ∞. For G = x² sin(1/x) (G(0) = 0):
     G′(x) = 2x sin(1/x) − cos(1/x) for x ≠ 0 and G′(0) = 0, so |G′| ≤ 3;
     by the MVT G is 3-Lipschitz, hence AC: given ε take δ = ε/3. (c) c is
     increasing, so BV with T = c(1) − c(0) = 1; it is continuous (no
     jumps, since its range is all of [0, 1]); it is constant on every
     removed interval, whose union has measure 1, so c′ = 0 a.e. If c were
     AC, the FTC would give c(1) − c(0) = ∫₀¹ c′ = 0 ≠ 1. Directly: C is
     covered by 2ⁿ intervals of total length (2/3)ⁿ < δ on which c
     increases by 1 in total. Its Stieltjes measure μ_c lives on C (m-null)
     and has no atoms: μ_c ⊥ m, singular continuous. Common mistakes:
     "continuous + differentiable a.e. ⇒ AC"; "BV ⇒ continuous". Now you
     try: T of x e⁻ˣ on [0, ∞). (2/e ≈ 0.73576: up to 1/e at x = 1, then
     back down to 0)

D12. (a) State the Riesz representation theorem for positive functionals
     on C_c(X) and its dual form for C₀(X). (b) For Λf = ∫₀¹ f(x) dx +
     f(1/2) on C_c(R) find μ and compute μ([0, 1/2]), μ((1/2, 1]),
     μ((0, 1)). (c) On C[0, 1] let Λf = f(0) − 2f(1) + ∫₀¹ x f(x) dx.
     Find the representing measure and ‖Λ‖; is the norm attained?
     Answer: (b) μ = m|[0,1] + δ_{1/2}; 3/2, 1/2, 2. (c) μ = δ₀ − 2δ₁ + x
     dx; ‖Λ‖ = |μ|([0, 1]) = 7/2, not attained.
     Teacher explains: (a) X locally compact Hausdorff, Λ : C_c(X) → R
     linear with Λf ≥ 0 when f ≥ 0. Then there is a UNIQUE Radon measure μ
     with Λf = ∫f dμ. CONSTRUCTION: for open U, μ(U) = sup{Λf : f ≺ U}
     (0 ≤ f ≤ 1, supp f ⊆ U compact); for any E, μ*(E) = inf{μ(U) : U ⊇
     E}. Urysohn's lemma produces the test functions, one checks that
     μ* is an outer measure whose Carathéodory sets contain the Borel
     sets, and that μ(K) = inf{Λf : f ≥ 𝟙_K}. DUAL FORM: every bounded
     linear Λ on C₀(X) is Λf = ∫f dμ for a unique regular complex Borel
     μ, and ‖Λ‖ = |μ|(X). (b) The integral part gives m|[0,1] and the
     evaluation gives δ_{1/2}, so μ = m|[0,1] + δ_{1/2}. Then μ([0, 1/2]) =
     1/2 + 1 = 3/2, μ((1/2, 1]) = 1/2 + 0 = 1/2, μ((0, 1)) = 1 + 1 = 2;
     check via the construction: bumps f ≺ (0, 1) with f(1/2) = 1 give
     Λf ↑ 2. (c) μ = δ₀ − 2δ₁ + x dx; the three pieces live on disjoint
     sets {0}, {1}, (0, 1), so |μ| = δ₀ + 2δ₁ + x dx and
        ‖Λ‖ = 1 + 2 + ∫₀¹ x dx = 7/2.
     "≤": |Λf| ≤ ‖f‖_∞|μ|([0, 1]). "≥": take continuous fₙ with ‖fₙ‖ = 1,
     fₙ(0) = 1, fₙ = −1 at 1 and fₙ = 1 on [0, 1 − 1/n]: Λfₙ → 1 + 2 + 1/2.
     Not attained: Λf = 7/2 with ‖f‖ ≤ 1 would force f = 1 on (0, 1) and
     f(1) = −1, impossible for continuous f. Common mistakes: forgetting
     that C[0, 1]* norms are TOTAL VARIATIONS (not |μ([0, 1])| = |1 − 2 +
     1/2| = 1/2); forgetting positivity in the first form.
     Now you try: the weak-* limit of μₙ = (1/n)∑_{k=1}^{n} δ_{k/n}.
     (m|[0,1]: Riemann sums ∫f dμₙ → ∫₀¹ f for every continuous f)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Outer measures: decide whether μ* is an outer measure; if so, find
    the Carathéodory σ-algebra 𝓜 ([P] = 1 if P is true, 0 otherwise).
    a) X = {1, 2}, μ*(∅) = 0, μ*(A) = 1 for A ≠ ∅
    b) X = {1, 2, 3}, μ*(A) = #A
    c) X = {1, 2, 3}, μ*(A) = min(#A, 2)
    d) X = {1, 2, 3}, μ*(A) = [A ∩ {1, 2} ≠ ∅] + [3 ∈ A]
    e) X = {1, 2, 3, 4}, μ*(A) = [A ∩ {1, 2} ≠ ∅] + [A ∩ {3, 4} ≠ ∅]
    f) X = N, μ*(A) = 0 if A is finite, 1 if A is infinite
    g) X = N, μ*(A) = 0 if A = ∅, 1 otherwise
    h) X uncountable, μ*(A) = 0 if A is countable, 1 otherwise
    i) X = [0, 1], μ*(A) = (m*(A))²
    j) X = [0, 1], μ*(A) = √(m*(A))
    k) X = R, μ* = m* + δ₀ (δ₀ defined on all subsets)
    l) X = {1, 2, 3}, ρ a premeasure on the algebra {∅, {1}, {2, 3}, X}
       with ρ{1} = 1, ρ{2, 3} = 2: the induced μ*({2}) and μ*({1, 2})
    m) X = R, Lebesgue m*: m*(Q ∩ [0, 1]) and m*([0, 1] ∖ Q)
    n) X = R, μ*(A) = m*(A ∩ [0, 1]): is [5, 6] ∪ V (V ⊆ [2, 3] a Vitali
       set translated there) in 𝓜?
    Answers:
      a) 𝓜 = {∅, X}        b) all subsets         c) 𝓜 = {∅, X}
      d) {∅, {3}, {1, 2}, X}                      e) {∅, {1,2}, {3,4}, X}
      f) not an outer measure (N = ⋃{n}: 1 ≰ 0)   g) {∅, N}
      h) the countable and co-countable sets
      i) not an outer measure: [0, 1] = [0, ½] ∪ [½, 1] gives 1 ≰ ¼ + ¼
      j) outer measure; 𝓜 = {E : m*(E) = 0 or m*([0, 1] ∖ E) = 0}
      k) 𝓜 = ℒ (δ₀ splits every set additively)
      l) 2 and 3            m) 0 and 1
      n) yes: it is μ*-null (every set outside [0, 1] is measurable)

M2. π-systems, λ-systems, uniqueness and extension.
    a) Is {(−∞, a] : a ∈ R} a π-system?    b) Is {[a, a + 1] : a ∈ R}?
    c) Is {∅} ∪ {open intervals} a π-system?
    d) On {1, 2, 3, 4}: is {∅, X, {1,2}, {3,4}, {1,3}, {2,4}} a λ-system?
       a σ-algebra?
    e) Must probability measures on {1, 2, 3, 4} agreeing on {1, 2} and
       {1, 3} be equal?
    f) Same, if they also agree on {1} and {4}?
    g) μ_F for F(x) = x³: μ((0, 2]) and μ((−1, 1] ∪ (2, 3])
    h) F(x) = arctan x: μ(R) and μ((0, ∞))
    i) F(x) = x + ⌊x⌋: μ((0, 2]), μ({1}), μ((0, 1))
    j) F(x) = eˣ: μ((−∞, 0])            k) F = 𝟙_[0,∞): which measure?
    l) Is counting measure on R σ-finite? on Z?
    m) μ = #(· ∩ Q), ν = 2#(· ∩ Q) on 𝔅(R): do they agree on all (a, b]?
       Are they equal?
    n) How many measures μ on 𝒫(N) have μ({n}) = 2⁻ⁿ for all n?
    o) Finite/cofinite algebra on N, ρ(A) = 0 (finite), 1 (cofinite): is
       ρ a premeasure?
    p) Is Lebesgue measure the unique translation-invariant Borel measure
       on R with m([0, 1]) = 1?
    Answers:
      a) yes      b) no ([0,1] ∩ [1,2] = {1})      c) yes
      d) λ-system yes; not a σ-algebra ({1,2} ∩ {1,3} = {1} missing)
      e) no: uniform vs ½(δ₁ + δ₄)        f) yes (they agree on every atom)
      g) 8 and 2 + 19 = 21                 h) π and π/2
      i) 4, 1, 1                           j) 1
      k) δ₀                                l) no; yes
      m) yes (both ∞ on non-empty intervals); no (μ{0} = 1, ν{0} = 2)
      n) exactly one: μ(A) = ∑_{n∈A} 2⁻ⁿ
      o) no: N = ⋃{n} disjoint, but 1 ≠ ∑0
      p) yes (among measures finite on compacts)

M3. Signed and complex measures: give a positive set P of a Hahn
    decomposition, ν⁺(X), ν⁻(X), |ν|(X).
    a) ν = 2δ₀ − 3δ₁ + δ₂               b) dν = x dx on [−1, 2]
    c) dν = sin x dx on [0, 2π]          d) dν = (x² − 1) dx on [−2, 2]
    e) dν = cos x dx on [0, 3π/2]        f) dν = (x − 1)e⁻ˣ dx on [0, ∞)
    g) dν = x e^(−x²) dx on R            h) ν = m|[0,1] − δ_{1/2}
    i) ν = m|[0,2] − m|[1,3]
    j) ν = ∑_{n≥1} (−1)ⁿ2⁻ⁿ δₙ on N (also give ν(N))
    k) dν = (x − ½) dx on [0, 1]
    l) ν = δ₀ − δ₁ on [0, 1]: all possible P
    m) complex dν = e^(ix) dx on [0, 2π]: |ν|(X) and ν(X)
    n) complex ν = (1 + i)δ₀ + 3iδ₁: |ν|(X)
    o) Is ν(A) = ∑_{n∈A} (−1)ⁿ/n on 𝒫(N) a signed measure?
    Answers:
      a) {0, 2}; 3, 3, 6               b) [0, 2]; 2, 1/2, 5/2
      c) [0, π]; 2, 2, 4               d) {|x| ≥ 1}; 8/3, 4/3, 4
      e) [0, π/2]; 1, 2, 3             f) [1, ∞); 1/e, 1/e, 2/e ≈ 0.73576
      g) [0, ∞); 1/2, 1/2, 1           h) [0, 1] ∖ {1/2}; 1, 1, 2
      i) [0, 1); 1, 1, 2 (Jordan is minimal: not 2, 2, 4)
      j) evens; 1/3, 2/3, 1; ν(N) = −1/3
      k) [1/2, 1]; 1/8, 1/8, 1/4
      l) any P with 0 ∈ P, 1 ∉ P (Hahn is unique only up to |ν|-null
         sets)
      m) 2π and 0                      n) √2 + 3 ≈ 4.41421
      o) no: ν(evens) = +∞ and ν(odds) = −∞

M4. For each pair write ν ≪ μ only, μ ≪ ν only, EQUIVALENT (both), ⊥, or
    NEITHER (m = Lebesgue measure).
    a) ν = e^(−x²) dx, μ = m on R        b) ν = δ₀, μ = m
    c) ν = m|[0,1], μ = m|[0,2]          d) ν = m|[0,1], μ = m|[1,2]
    e) ν = m|[0,2], μ = m|[1,3]          f) ν = Cantor measure, μ = m|[0,1]
    g) ν = ∑2⁻ⁿδ_{qₙ} (qₙ all rationals), μ = m
    h) ν = counting on Z, μ = ∑_{n∈Z} 2^(−|n|)δₙ
    i) ν = m, μ = counting measure on R
    j) ν = N(0, 1), μ = Cauchy           k) ν = N(0, 1), μ = N(5, 4)
    l) ν = uniform[0, 1], μ = Exp(1)     m) ν = δ₀ + m|[0,1], μ = m|[0,1]
    n) ν = m² on [0, 1]², μ = length on the diagonal
    o) ν = law of U², μ = law of U (U uniform on [0, 1])
    p) ν = Bin(3, ½), μ = Poisson(1)
    Answers:
      a) equivalent        b) ⊥             c) ν ≪ μ only
      d) ⊥ (they share only {1}, a null set for both)
      e) neither           f) ⊥             g) ⊥
      h) equivalent        i) ν ≪ μ only (no density: μ not σ-finite)
      j) equivalent        k) equivalent    l) ν ≪ μ only
      m) μ ≪ ν only        n) ⊥             o) equivalent
      p) ν ≪ μ only (Poisson charges {4, 5, ...})

M5. Radon-Nikodym derivatives: find dν/dμ (or say none exists).
    a) μ = m on [0, 1], ν(A) = ∫_A 5x⁴ dx
    b) μ = m on R, ν = N(0, 1)          c) μ = N(0, 1), ν = m
    d) μ = N(0, 1), ν = N(1, 1)         e) μ = e⁻ˣdx, ν = 2e^(−2x)dx on
                                           [0, ∞)
    f) μ = ∑_{n≥1} 2⁻ⁿδₙ, ν = ∑_{n≥1} 3⁻ⁿδₙ
    g) μ = δ₀ + δ₁ + 2δ₂, ν = 3δ₀ + δ₂
    h) μ = m on (0, 1), ν = law of x² under m on (0, 1)
    i) μ = m on (0, ∞), ν = law of −ln U (U uniform on (0, 1))
    j) μ = m on [0, 1], ν = μ_F with F(x) = x² + x
    k) μ = m, ν = μ_F with F = arctan
    l) dλ/dν = 2x, dν/dμ = 3x²: dλ/dμ
    m) dν/dμ = 1 + x²: dμ/dν
    n) μ = m² on [0, 1]², ν = 2m²|_{x<y}
    o) μ = Poisson(1), ν = Poisson(λ) on {0, 1, 2, ...}
    p) μ = Bin(2, ½), ν = Bin(2, p) on {0, 1, 2}
    q) μ = m on [0, 1], ν = δ_{1/2}
    Answers:
      a) 5x⁴            b) e^(−x²/2)/√(2π)     c) √(2π) e^(x²/2)
      d) e^(x − 1/2)    e) 2e⁻ˣ                f) (2/3)ⁿ at n
      g) 3 at 0, 0 at 1, 1/2 at 2               h) 1/(2√y)
      i) e^(−y) on (0, ∞) (Exp(1))              j) 2x + 1
      k) 1/(1 + x²)     l) 6x³                 m) 1/(1 + x²)
      n) 2𝟙_{x<y}       o) λᵏe^(1−λ) at k
      p) 4(1 − p)², 4p(1 − p), 4p² at 0, 1, 2
      q) none: ν({1/2}) = 1 but m({1/2}) = 0

M6. Lebesgue decomposition relative to m on R: give the absolutely
    continuous part (as a density), the pure point part and the singular
    continuous part (write 0 if absent).
    a) μ_F, F(x) = max(0, min(x, 1))    b) μ_F, F = 𝟙_[0,∞)
    c) μ_F, F(x) = ⌊x⌋                   d) μ_F, F(x) = x + ⌊x⌋
    e) μ_F, F = Cantor function (0 on (−∞, 0], 1 on [1, ∞))
    f) μ_F, F = Cantor function + max(0, min(x, 1))
    g) μ_F, F(x) = x² for x ≥ 0, 0 for x < 0, plus 3·𝟙_[1,∞)(x)
    h) law of max(U, 1/2), U uniform on [0, 1]
    i) law of U·B, U uniform[0, 1], B independent, P(B = 0) = P(B = 1) = ½
    j) ½N(0, 1) + ½δ₀
    k) law of ∑ εₙ2⁻ⁿ, εₙ independent fair bits
    l) law of ∑ 2εₙ3⁻ⁿ, εₙ independent fair bits
    m) law of ∑ εₙ2⁻ⁿ, P(εₙ = 1) = 1/3
    n) sin²x dx + δ_π
    o) law of U + ⌊2U⌋ (U uniform on [0, 1])
    p) (in R²) m² + arc length on the unit circle: which part is singular?
    Answers:
      a) 𝟙_[0,1]; 0; 0                  b) 0; δ₀; 0
      c) 0; ∑_{n∈Z} δₙ; 0              d) 1; ∑_{n∈Z} δₙ; 0
      e) 0; 0; μ_c                      f) 𝟙_[0,1]; 0; μ_c
      g) 2x𝟙_{x≥0}; 3δ₁; 0              h) 𝟙_(1/2,1]; ½δ_{1/2}; 0
      i) ½𝟙_[0,1]; ½δ₀; 0               j) ½ e^(−x²/2)/√(2π); ½δ₀; 0
      k) 𝟙_[0,1] (uniform); 0; 0        l) 0; 0; Cantor measure
      m) 0; 0; singular continuous (lives on the numbers whose binary
         digits have frequency 1/3 of ones — a null set by the SLLN)
      n) sin²x; δ_π; 0
      o) 𝟙_[0,1/2) + 𝟙_[3/2,2); 0; 0 (a jump in the map, not in the law)
      p) arc length (⊥ m², no atoms)

M7. Hardy-Littlewood maximal functions on R: Mf(x) = sup_{r>0} (1/2r)
    ∫_{x−r}^{x+r}|f| (centred) unless stated; f = 𝟙_[0,1] unless stated.
    a) Mf(3)          b) Mf(1/2)        c) Mf(0)          d) Mf(−1)
    e) m{Mf > 1/4}    f) m{Mf > 3/4}    g) m{Mf > 1/10}
    h) the uncentred M̃f(3) (sup over all intervals containing 3)
    i) f = 𝟙_[−1,1]: Mf(3)
    j) f = |x|^(−1/2)𝟙_{|x|≤1}: Mf(0)
    k) f ≡ c (constant): Mf            l) f(x) = x: Mf
    m) ‖Mf‖₂² (compare ‖f‖₂² = 1)
    n) sup_{t>0} t·m{Mf > t} (the weak-L¹ size of Mf)
    o) the dyadic maximal function of 𝟙_[0,1) at x = 3
    p) Is Mf ∈ L¹(R) for some non-zero f ∈ L¹(R)?
    q) Is {Mf > t} always open?        r) Is Mf ≥ |f| a.e.?
    Answers:
      a) 1/6       b) 1         c) 1/2       d) 1/4
      e) 3 (the interval (−1, 2))       f) 1     g) 9
      h) 1/3 (interval [0, 3])          i) 1/4 (in general a/(|x| + a))
      j) ∞ (averages 2/√r)              k) |c|   l) ≡ ∞
      m) 1 + 2∫₁^∞ dx/(4x²) = 3/2       n) 1 (= ‖f‖₁; not attained)
      o) 1/4 (the interval [0, 4))      p) no (Mf ≥ c/|x| at infinity)
      q) yes (Mf is lower semicontinuous)
      r) yes (Lebesgue differentiation theorem)

M8. Lebesgue points, densities and derivatives of measures (limits as
    r → 0⁺; balls/intervals centred at the given point).
    a) (1/2r)∫_{−r}^{r} cos x dx      b) (1/2r)∫_{−r}^{r} 𝟙_[0,∞)
    c) Is 0 a Lebesgue point of 𝟙_[0,∞)?
    d) Is 0 a Lebesgue point of 𝟙_Q (value 1 at 0)? of the function 0?
    e) density of [0, 1] at 0; at 1/2
    f) density of {|y| < x} ⊆ R² at the origin
    g) density of {y > x²} at the origin   h) density of {|y| < x²}
    i) density of the Cantor set at a point of the Cantor set
    j) density of a fat Cantor set F at a.e. point of F
    k) ν = δ₀ + m: ν(x − r, x + r)/(2r) at x = 0; at x = 1
    l) Cantor measure μ_c: μ_c([x − r, x + r])/(2r) at x = 1/2; at x = 0
       along r = 3⁻ᵏ
    m) (1/πr²)∬_{B(0,r)} e^(x+y) dA
    n) (1/h)∫₁^{1+h} 𝟙_[0,1] as h → 0⁺; (1/h)∫_{1−h}^{1} 𝟙_[0,1] as h → 0⁺
    o) (1/2r)∫_{−r}^{r} |sin(1/x)| dx
    p) E = ⋃_{k≥0} (2^(−2k−1), 2^(−2k)]: the ratio m(E ∩ (0, r))/r at
       r = 4⁻ᵏ and at r = 2·4⁻ᵏ⁻¹; does the density at 0 exist?
    q) ν = x² dx + δ₁: the derivative Dν(x) = lim ν(B(x, r))/(2r)
    Answers:
      a) 1         b) 1/2       c) no (a jump)
      d) no (averages of |f − 1| are 1); yes — Lebesgue points depend on
         the representative
      e) 1/2; 1    f) 1/4       g) 1/2       h) 0
      i) 0 (m(C) = 0)           j) 1 (though F contains no interval)
      k) ∞; 1      l) 0; (3/2)ᵏ/2 → ∞        m) 1
      n) 0; 1      o) 2/π ≈ 0.63662
      p) 2/3 and 1/3: the density does not exist
      q) x² for x ≠ 1, ∞ at x = 1

M9. Total variation T_F on the given interval; then classify: AC
    (absolutely continuous), BV but not AC, or not BV.
    a) x² on [−1, 2]                  b) sin x on [0, 2π]
    c) x³ − 3x on [−2, 2]              d) ⌊x⌋ on [0, 3.5]
    e) the Cantor function on [0, 1]   f) √x on [0, 1]
    g) x sin(1/x) on [0, 1]            h) x² sin(1/x) on [0, 1]
    i) x² sin(1/x²) on [0, 1]          j) 𝟙_Q on [0, 1]
    k) |x| on [−1, 1]                  l) x e⁻ˣ on [0, ∞)
    m) cos(πx) on [0, 3]               n) x^(1/3) on [−1, 1]
    o) ∫₀ˣ sgn(sin(1/t)) dt on [0, 1]
    p) F(x) = ∑_{qₙ<x} 2⁻ⁿ on [0, 1] (qₙ an enumeration of Q ∩ (0, 1))
    q) the positive and negative variations of sin x on [0, 2π]
    Answers:
      a) 5, AC          b) 4, AC           c) 12, AC
      d) 3, BV not AC (jumps)              e) 1, BV not AC (continuous!)
      f) 1, AC (not Lipschitz)             g) not BV (∑1/k diverges)
      h) finite, AC (|F′| ≤ 3: Lipschitz)
      i) not BV (differentiable everywhere, but ∫|F′| = ∞)
      j) not BV         k) 2, AC           l) 2/e ≈ 0.73576, AC
      m) 6, AC          n) 2, AC           o) 1, AC (|F′| = 1 a.e.)
      p) 1, BV not AC (increasing, discontinuous at every rational,
         F′ = 0 a.e.)
      q) 2 and 2

M10. Riesz representation: find the (signed/complex) measure μ with Λf =
     ∫f dμ and the requested quantities (m = Lebesgue measure).
     a) Λf = f(2) on C_c(R)
     b) Λf = ∫₀¹ f dx + f(1/2): μ([0, 1/2])
     c) Λf = ∑_{n≥1} 2⁻ⁿf(1/n) on C[0, 1]: μ({1}), μ([0, 1/3])
     d) Λf = ∫₀¹ f(x²) dx on C[0, 1]: the density; μ([0, 1/4])
     e) Λf = ∫₀¹ f(c(x)) dx, c the Cantor function: μ({1/2}), μ({1/4})
     f) Λf = ∫_R f(x) e^(−x²) dx: μ(R)
     g) Λf = f(0)² on C_c(R)            h) Λf = f′(0) on C_c¹(R)
     i) Λf = ∫₀¹ f dx − f(1) on C[0, 1]: ‖Λ‖
     j) Λf = f(0) − 2f(1) + ∫₀¹ x f(x) dx: ‖Λ‖
     k) Λf = ∫₀¹ f(x) sin(2πx) dx: ‖Λ‖
     l) Λf = ∫₀^{2π} f(x) e^(ix) dx (complex): ‖Λ‖
     m) Λf = (f(0) + f(1))/2 − f(1/2): ‖Λ‖
     n) Λf = ∫₀¹ f dF (Riemann-Stieltjes), F(x) = x + 𝟙_[1/2,1](x): μ,
        ‖Λ‖
     o) Λf = ∫_R f dx: μ((0, 1)) = sup{Λf : f ≺ (0, 1)} and μ([0, 1]) =
        inf{Λf : f ≥ 𝟙_[0,1]}
     Answers:
       a) δ₂              b) m|[0,1] + δ_{1/2}; 3/2
       c) ∑2⁻ⁿδ_{1/n}; 1/2, 1/4
       d) 1/(2√y) on (0, 1]; 1/2
       e) purely atomic (the plateaus of c): 1/3, 1/9
       f) e^(−x²) dx; √π ≈ 1.77245
       g) none (not linear)
       h) none (not bounded in the sup norm; a distribution of order 1)
       i) m − δ₁; 2       j) δ₀ − 2δ₁ + x dx; 7/2
       k) 2/π ≈ 0.63662   l) 2π              m) 2
       n) m|[0,1] + δ_{1/2}; 2                o) 1 and 1

M11. Weak-* convergence of measures on R (test functions f ∈ C₀(R)):
     find the limit of μₙ, or the requested limit.
     a) δ_{1/n}                          b) δₙ
     c) (1/n)∑_{k=1}^{n} δ_{k/n}         d) n𝟙_[0,1/n] dx
     e) (1/2n)𝟙_[−n,n] dx                f) N(0, 1/n)
     g) (1 + sin nx)𝟙_[0,2π] dx          h) n xⁿ⁻¹𝟙_[0,1] dx
     i) ½(δ_{−1/n} + δ_{1/n})            j) n(δ_{1/n} − δ₀)
     k) normalised Lebesgue measure on the n-th Cantor stage Cₙ
     l) Bin(n, λ/n)
     m) lim ∫x² dμₙ for μₙ as in c)
     n) lim ∫cos x dμₙ for μₙ = N(0, 1/n)
     o) μₙ = (1 − 1/n)δ₀ + (1/n)δₙ: the limit, and lim ∫x dμₙ
     p) μₙ = law of sin(nU), U uniform on [0, 2π]
     q) Does every sequence of probability measures on [0, 1] have a
        weak-* convergent subsequence? Is the limit a probability
        measure?
     Answers:
       a) δ₀        b) 0 (mass escapes to ∞)     c) m|[0,1]
       d) δ₀        e) 0                         f) δ₀
       g) 𝟙_[0,2π] dx (Riemann-Lebesgue)          h) δ₁
       i) δ₀
       j) no limit measure (‖μₙ‖ = 2n); ∫f dμₙ → f′(0) for f ∈ C¹ — the
          distribution −δ₀′
       k) the Cantor measure μ_c          l) Poisson(λ)
       m) 1/3       n) 1 (= lim e^(−1/(2n)))
       o) δ₀ (even narrowly); ∫x dμₙ = 1 for all n but ∫x dδ₀ = 0
       p) the arcsine law dy/(π√(1 − y²)) on (−1, 1) for every n
       q) yes (Helly, or Banach-Alaoglu with C[0, 1]* = M[0, 1]); yes,
          because [0, 1] is compact (f ≡ 1 is a test function)

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) The Carathéodory measure of an outer measure is complete.
     b) Two measures on σ(𝒫) agreeing on a π-system 𝒫 are equal.
     c) Two finite measures agreeing on a π-system 𝒫 ∋ X are equal on
        σ(𝒫).
     d) If ν ≪ μ then ν = f dμ for some measurable f ≥ 0.
     e) If ν ≪ μ, both σ-finite, dν/dμ is unique μ-a.e.
     f) ν ≪ μ ⇔ the ε-δ condition, for every measure ν.
     g) A measure without atoms is absolutely continuous w.r.t. m.
     h) Every finite Borel measure on R is regular.
     i) If f ∈ L¹(R) then Mf ∈ L¹(R).
     j) A continuous increasing F with F′ = 0 a.e. is constant.
     k) Every everywhere-differentiable F on [0, 1] is AC.
     l) If F is differentiable everywhere on [a, b] and F′ ∈ L¹, then
        F(b) − F(a) = ∫ₐᵇ F′.
     m) Every Lipschitz function is AC.
     n) Every AC function on [0, 1] is Lipschitz.
     o) The composition of two AC functions on [0, 1] is AC.
     p) (L^∞[0, 1])* = L¹[0, 1].
     q) (L¹(μ))* = L^∞(μ) for every measure μ.
     r) C₀(R)* is isometric to the finite signed Radon measures with the
        total variation norm.
     s) A weak-* limit in C₀(R)* of probability measures is a
        probability measure.
     t) Every λ-system is a σ-algebra.
     u) At a.e. point of R a measurable set E has density 0 or 1.
     v) If μ is finite Borel and μ ⊥ m, then μ(x − r, x + r)/(2r) → 0 for
        m-a.e. x.
     Answers:
       a) T
       b) F: #(· ∩ Q) and 2#(· ∩ Q) on the intervals (a, b]
       c) T
       d) F: m ≪ counting measure on [0, 1] (not σ-finite)
       e) T         f) F: dν = dx/x on (0, 1)
       g) F: the Cantor measure         h) T
       i) F: 𝟙_[0,1] (Mf ~ 1/(2|x|))   j) F: the Cantor function
       k) F: x² sin(1/x²) (not BV)
       l) T (a theorem; the hard case of the FTC)
       m) T         n) F: √x
       o) F: √x ∘ x² sin²(1/x) = |x sin(1/x)|, not BV
       p) F: a Hahn-Banach extension of f ↦ f(0) (D8)
       q) F: X = {a}, μ{a} = ∞: L¹ = {0} but L^∞ = R
       r) T (Riesz)  s) F: δₙ → 0     t) F: M2 d)
       u) T (Lebesgue density theorem)
       v) T (singular parts have derivative 0 a.e.)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: keep three pictures on the board — a DENSITY (a smooth hill,
  ≪ m), an ATOM (a spike δₓ) and the CANTOR "devil's staircase" (a
  continuous measure living on a null set) — and classify every measure
  into these three pieces before computing anything; most Radon-Nikodym
  and Lebesgue-decomposition questions are then just "read off the
  density, list the atoms, look for a Cantor-like remainder". Insist on
  checking σ-finiteness before quoting uniqueness or Radon-Nikodym, and
  finiteness before quoting ε-δ or continuity from above. For total
  variations, split into monotone pieces or use ∫|F′| only after checking
  AC. For Riesz, always ask "which points are evaluated, which parts are
  integrated?" and remember that dual norms are total variations. Harder:
  prove the Hahn decomposition via the lemma in D4 in full detail; prove
  Riesz-Markov-Kakutani from Urysohn's lemma and partitions of unity;
  prove the Besicovitch or Vitali covering theorem for Radon measures and
  deduce differentiation of arbitrary Radon measures on Rⁿ; show that a
  function F is AC iff it is continuous, BV and maps null sets to null
  sets (Banach-Zarecki); prove (Lᵖ)* = L^q without R-N via uniform
  convexity; and show that M(X) is the dual of C₀(X) but C₀(X) is not
  reflexive unless X is finite.


================================================================================
TOPIC 12.2 — FUNCTIONAL ANALYSIS II: WEAK TOPOLOGIES, COMPACT OPERATORS,
SPECTRAL THEORY, BANACH ALGEBRAS, DISTRIBUTIONS AND SOBOLEV SPACES
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - WEAK TOPOLOGY σ(X, X*): the weakest topology making every f ∈ X*
    continuous; xₙ ⇀ x ⇔ f(xₙ) → f(x) for all f ∈ X*. Weakly convergent
    sequences are BOUNDED (uniform boundedness) and ‖x‖ ≤ lim inf ‖xₙ‖
    (weak lower semicontinuity of the norm). Standard examples: eₙ ⇀ 0 in
    ℓᵖ (1 < p < ∞) and c₀ but not in norm; sin(nx) ⇀ 0 in L²(0, 2π)
    (Riemann-Lebesgue); in a Hilbert space xₙ ⇀ x and ‖xₙ‖ → ‖x‖ ⇒
    xₙ → x. SCHUR: in ℓ¹ weak and norm convergence of SEQUENCES coincide.
    In infinite dimension the weak topology is not metrisable and the
    weak closure of the unit sphere is the closed unit ball.
  - MAZUR: a convex set is norm-closed iff weakly closed; hence a weak
    limit is a norm limit of CONVEX COMBINATIONS (e.g. (e₁ + ... + eₙ)/n
    → 0 in ℓ², norm 1/√n). Weakly lower semicontinuous convex functionals
    attain their minimum on weakly compact sets (direct method).
  - WEAK* TOPOLOGY σ(X*, X): fₙ →* f ⇔ fₙ(x) → f(x) for every x ∈ X.
    BANACH-ALAOGLU: the closed unit ball of X* is weak* compact (weak*
    SEQUENTIALLY compact if X is separable). δ_{1/n} →* δ₀ in C[0, 1]* but
    ‖δ_{1/n} − δ₀‖ = 2. Duals: c₀* = ℓ¹, (ℓ¹)* = ℓ^∞, (ℓᵖ)* = ℓ^q, (L¹)* =
    L^∞, C(K)* = M(K) (Riesz), (ℓ^∞)* ⊋ ℓ¹.
  - REFLEXIVITY: J : X → X** onto. X is reflexive ⇔ its closed unit ball
    is weakly compact (Kakutani) ⇔ every bounded sequence has a weakly
    convergent subsequence (Eberlein-Šmulian). Reflexive: Hilbert spaces,
    ℓᵖ, Lᵖ, W^{k,p} (1 < p < ∞), finite-dimensional spaces. Not: c₀, ℓ¹,
    ℓ^∞, C[0, 1], L¹, L^∞. GOLDSTINE: J(B_X) is weak* dense in B_{X**}.
  - COMPACT OPERATORS: T ∈ K(X, Y) if T maps bounded sets to relatively
    compact sets (⇔ every bounded (xₙ) has (Txₙ) with a convergent
    subsequence). K(X) is a closed two-sided ideal in B(X); finite-rank
    operators and their norm limits are compact (on a Hilbert space these
    are ALL the compact operators); SCHAUDER: T compact ⇔ T* compact.
    Compact operators map weakly convergent sequences to norm convergent
    ones. diag(dₙ) on ℓ² is compact ⇔ dₙ → 0; I is compact ⇔ dim < ∞.
  - HILBERT-SCHMIDT OPERATORS: ∑‖Teₖ‖² < ∞; an integral operator with
    kernel k ∈ L²(Ω × Ω) is Hilbert-Schmidt with ‖T‖ ≤ ‖T‖_HS =
    ‖k‖_{L²}, hence compact. Volterra Vf(x) = ∫₀ˣ f: compact, ‖V‖ = 2/π,
    ‖V‖_HS = 1/√2, σ(V) = {0}, no eigenvalues (quasinilpotent).
  - RIESZ-SCHAUDER THEORY: for T compact on a Banach space and λ ≠ 0,
    ker(T − λ) is finite-dimensional, ran(T − λ) is closed, and
    FREDHOLM ALTERNATIVE: T − λ is injective ⇔ surjective; the equation
    (λ − T)f = g is solvable ⇔ g ⊥ ker(λ̄ − T*). σ(T) ∖ {0} consists of
    eigenvalues of finite multiplicity accumulating at most at 0; 0 ∈ σ(T)
    if dim X = ∞. FREDHOLM OPERATORS: dim ker, codim ran finite; index
    ind(A) = dim ker A − codim ran A is stable under compact perturbation
    (ind(I − K) = 0, ind(right shift) = −1).
  - SPECTRUM, FINER: σ(T) = σ_p (T − λ not injective) ∪ σ_c (injective,
    range dense but not all of X) ∪ σ_r (injective, range not dense).
    Right shift S on ℓ²: σ = D̄, σ_p = ∅, σ_r = open disc D, σ_c = unit
    circle; left shift: σ_p = D; M_x on L²[0, 1]: σ = σ_c = [0, 1]. For
    normal T: r(T) = ‖T‖, σ_r = ∅, and ‖(T − λ)⁻¹‖ = 1/dist(λ, σ(T)).
  - SPECTRAL THEOREM (COMPACT SELF-ADJOINT): H has an orthonormal basis of
    eigenvectors, T = ∑λₖ⟨·, eₖ⟩eₖ with real λₖ → 0, ‖T‖ = max|λₖ|
    (min-max / Rayleigh quotient); for a continuous positive kernel,
    MERCER: k(x, y) = ∑λₖeₖ(x)eₖ(y) and ∑λₖ = ∫k(x, x) dx. Example
    min(x, y) on [0, 1]: λₖ = 1/((k − ½)²π²), eₖ = √2 sin((k − ½)πx).
  - SPECTRAL THEOREM (BOUNDED SELF-ADJOINT / NORMAL): T is unitarily
    equivalent to a multiplication operator M_φ on some L²(μ); equivalently
    T = ∫λ dE(λ) for a projection-valued measure E on σ(T). CONTINUOUS
    FUNCTIONAL CALCULUS f ↦ f(T), an isometric *-homomorphism C(σ(T)) →
    B(H) with ‖f(T)‖ = sup_{σ(T)}|f|; SPECTRAL MAPPING σ(f(T)) = f(σ(T)).
    Positive square root, |T| = (T*T)^(1/2), polar decomposition T = U|T|.
    Unbounded self-adjoint operators (−d²/dx² on L²(R): σ = [0, ∞)) have
    real spectrum; −i d/dx on L²(0, 2π) periodic: σ = Z.
  - BANACH ALGEBRAS: complex algebra with ‖ab‖ ≤ ‖a‖‖b‖, unit ‖1‖ = 1.
    The invertibles G(A) are open (1 − a invertible if ‖a‖ < 1, Neumann
    series); σ(a) is compact and NON-EMPTY (Liouville applied to the
    resolvent); SPECTRAL RADIUS FORMULA r(a) = lim ‖aⁿ‖^(1/n) =
    inf ‖aⁿ‖^(1/n). GELFAND-MAZUR: a complex Banach division algebra is C
    (real: R, C, H). The spectrum depends on the algebra: z has spectrum
    D̄ in the disc algebra A(D) but the circle T in C(T).
  - GELFAND THEORY (commutative, unital): characters φ : A → C ↔ maximal
    ideals ker φ; the maximal ideal space Δ is weak* compact; the GELFAND
    TRANSFORM â(φ) = φ(a) satisfies σ(a) = â(Δ), ‖â‖_∞ = r(a). Examples:
    C(K): Δ = K; ℓ¹(Z) (convolution): Δ = T, â = ∑aₙzⁿ; ℓ¹(N₀): Δ = D̄;
    L¹(R) (no unit): Δ = R, â = Fourier transform. WIENER'S LEMMA: a
    non-vanishing absolutely convergent Fourier series has an absolutely
    convergent reciprocal.
  - C*-ALGEBRAS: Banach *-algebra with ‖a*a‖ = ‖a‖². r(a) = ‖a‖ for normal
    a; spectrum does not depend on the C*-subalgebra (spectral
    permanence). GELFAND-NAIMARK: a commutative unital C*-algebra is
    isometrically *-isomorphic to C(Δ); every C*-algebra is a closed
    *-subalgebra of some B(H). ℓ¹(Z) is a Banach *-algebra but not C*.
  - DISTRIBUTIONS: test functions D(Ω) = C_c^∞(Ω) with the inductive-limit
    topology; T ∈ D′(Ω) is linear with |T(φ)| ≤ C_K ∑_{|α|≤N}‖∂^αφ‖_∞ on
    each compact K (ORDER ≤ N). Regular distributions T_f (f ∈ L¹_loc),
    δ (order 0), δ′ (order 1), p.v. 1/x (order 1). DERIVATIVE ⟨∂^αT, φ⟩ =
    (−1)^{|α|}⟨T, ∂^αφ⟩, always defined: H′ = δ, |x|″ = 2δ,
    (log|x|)′ = p.v. 1/x. Multiplication by C^∞ functions: xδ = 0,
    xδ′ = −δ, x · p.v. 1/x = 1; products of two distributions are not
    defined in general. SUPPORT; distributions supported at {0} are
    ∑_{finite} cₖδ^(k); compactly supported ones have finite order.
    Convergence in D′ is pointwise on test functions: n𝟙_[0,1/n] → δ,
    sin(nx) → 0, sin(nx)/(πx) → δ.
  - TEMPERED DISTRIBUTIONS AND FOURIER: S(Rⁿ) Schwartz space, S′ its dual;
    f̂(ξ) = ∫f(x)e^(−ix·ξ) dx extends to S′ by ⟨T̂, φ⟩ = ⟨T, φ̂⟩: δ̂ = 1,
    1̂ = 2πδ, (∂T)^ = iξT̂, (xT)^ = i∂_ξT̂, (sign x)^ = −2i p.v. 1/ξ,
    (e^(−|x|))^ = 2/(1 + ξ²). PLANCHEREL ‖f̂‖₂² = 2π‖f‖₂² (n = 1).
    FUNDAMENTAL SOLUTIONS PE = δ: d/dx: H; d²/dx²: |x|/2; −d²/dx² + k²:
    e^(−k|x|)/(2k); Δ in R³: −1/(4π|x|); Δ in R²: (1/2π) log|x|; then
    u = E * f solves Pu = f. MALGRANGE-EHRENPREIS: every constant-
    coefficient P ≠ 0 has one.
  - SOBOLEV SPACES: W^{k,p}(Ω) = {u ∈ Lᵖ : ∂^αu ∈ Lᵖ (weak derivatives)
    for |α| ≤ k}, a Banach space (Hilbert for p = 2, H^k = W^{k,2});
    H^s(Rⁿ) = {u : (1 + |ξ|²)^(s/2)û ∈ L²} for real s; W^{k,p}_0 = closure
    of C_c^∞. |x|^α ∈ W^{1,p}(B₁ ⊂ Rⁿ) ⇔ α > 1 − n/p (or α = 0). Meyers-
    Serrin: C^∞ ∩ W^{k,p} is dense. In 1D, W^{1,p}(a, b) functions are
    absolutely continuous; the Heaviside function is not in W^{1,1}.
  - SOBOLEV INEQUALITIES: GAGLIARDO-NIRENBERG-SOBOLEV W^{1,p}(Rⁿ) ⊂ L^{p*},
    1/p* = 1/p − 1/n (p < n); MORREY W^{1,p} ⊂ C^{0,1−n/p} (p > n);
    H^s(Rⁿ) ⊂ C₀ for s > n/2; the borderline p = n fails
    (log log(e/|x|) ∈ H¹(B₁ ⊂ R²) is unbounded). RELLICH-KONDRACHOV:
    H¹₀(Ω) ⊂ L²(Ω) is COMPACT for bounded Ω (false on R: translates).
    POINCARÉ: ‖u‖₂ ≤ C‖∇u‖₂ on H¹₀(Ω), best C = λ₁^(−1/2) (L/π on
    (0, L)). TRACE: H¹(Ω) → H^(1/2)(∂Ω) ⊂ L²(∂Ω). With LAX-MILGRAM these
    give weak solutions of −Δu = f, u ∈ H¹₀ (see 12.13).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Show that the unit vectors eₙ converge weakly to 0 in ℓ² but not
    in norm, and that they do not converge weakly in ℓ¹. (b) Show that
    sin(nx) ⇀ 0 and sin²(nx) ⇀ 1/2 in L²(0, 2π). (c) Prove that
    ‖x‖ ≤ lim inf ‖xₙ‖ when xₙ ⇀ x, and that in a Hilbert space xₙ ⇀ x
    together with ‖xₙ‖ → ‖x‖ implies xₙ → x. (d) Illustrate Mazur's lemma
    with eₙ.
    Answer: (a) ⟨eₙ, y⟩ = yₙ → 0, ‖eₙ − eₘ‖ = √2; in ℓ¹ test with
    (1, 1, 1, ...) ∈ ℓ^∞. (b) Riemann-Lebesgue. (c) Hahn-Banach and
    ‖xₙ − x‖² = ‖xₙ‖² − 2Re⟨xₙ, x⟩ + ‖x‖² → 0. (d) ‖(e₁ + ... + eₙ)/n‖₂ =
    1/√n → 0.
    Teacher explains: (a) (ℓ²)* = ℓ² by Riesz, so eₙ ⇀ 0 means ⟨eₙ, y⟩ =
    yₙ → 0 for every y ∈ ℓ², which holds because ∑|yₙ|² < ∞. But
    ‖eₙ‖ = 1 ↛ 0, and ‖eₙ − eₘ‖ = √2 shows no subsequence is even Cauchy.
    In ℓ¹ the dual is ℓ^∞; the functional f(x) = ∑xₖ, given by (1, 1, ...),
    has f(eₙ) = 1, so the only possible weak limit is not 0; but testing
    against the coordinate functionals forces the limit to be 0. So no
    weak limit exists (Schur: in ℓ¹ weak convergence of a sequence is
    norm convergence, and eₙ is not norm Cauchy). (b) For g ∈ L²(0, 2π) ⊂
    L¹, ∫g(x) sin(nx) dx → 0 by the Riemann-Lebesgue lemma (or Bessel's
    inequality: these are Fourier coefficients of g). Note ‖sin nx‖₂ = √π:
    no norm convergence. sin²(nx) = ½ − ½cos(2nx) ⇀ ½. The weak limit of
    a square is NOT the square of the weak limit (0² ≠ ½): weak limits
    average out oscillations. (c) By Hahn-Banach choose f ∈ X* with
    ‖f‖ = 1, f(x) = ‖x‖. Then ‖x‖ = lim f(xₙ) ≤ lim inf ‖xₙ‖. In a
    Hilbert space, ‖xₙ − x‖² = ‖xₙ‖² − 2Re⟨xₙ, x⟩ + ‖x‖² → ‖x‖² − 2‖x‖² +
    ‖x‖² = 0. (d) Mazur: a closed convex set is weakly closed, so 0 (a
    weak limit of eₙ) lies in the norm closure of conv{eₙ, eₙ₊₁, ...};
    concretely ‖(e₁ + ... + eₙ)/n‖₂ = √n/n = 1/√n → 0.
    Common mistakes: thinking weak convergence preserves products or
    norms; forgetting that in ℓ¹ one must use the dual ℓ^∞, not ℓ¹.
    Now you try: find the weak limit of |sin(nx)| in L²(0, π). (the
    average (1/π)∫₀^π |sin x| dx = 2/π)

D2. (a) State the Banach-Alaoglu theorem. (b) Show δ_{1/n} →* δ₀ in
    C[0, 1]* but not in norm. (c) Show that eₙ →* 0 in ℓ¹ = c₀*. (d) Use
    the sequence sₙ = e₁ + ... + eₙ to prove that c₀ is not reflexive.
    Answer: (a) B_{X*} is weak* compact. (b) f(1/n) → f(0) for f ∈ C[0, 1],
    ‖δ_{1/n} − δ₀‖ = 2. (c) ⟨eₙ, x⟩ = xₙ → 0 for x ∈ c₀. (d) (sₙ) is
    bounded but has no weakly convergent subsequence in c₀.
    Teacher explains: (a) BANACH-ALAOGLU: for any normed X, the closed
    unit ball of X* is compact in the weak* topology. Proof idea: B_{X*}
    embeds in the product ∏_{x∈X} [−‖x‖, ‖x‖] (Tychonoff) as a closed
    set. If X is separable, the ball is weak* metrisable, so every
    bounded sequence in X* has a weak* convergent subsequence.
    (b) C[0, 1]* = M[0, 1] (Riesz). For f ∈ C[0, 1], ⟨δ_{1/n}, f⟩ = f(1/n) →
    f(0) by continuity: weak* convergence. In norm, ‖δ_a − δ_b‖ = |δ_a −
    δ_b|([0, 1]) = 2 for a ≠ b (take f continuous, |f| ≤ 1, f(a) = 1,
    f(b) = −1). (c) Here the predual is c₀, so we only test against x ∈
    c₀: ⟨eₙ, x⟩ = xₙ → 0. So eₙ →* 0 in ℓ¹, although (D1) not weakly —
    weak* is genuinely weaker than weak. (d) ‖sₙ‖_∞ = 1. For a ∈ ℓ¹ =
    c₀*, ⟨a, sₙ⟩ = a₁ + ... + aₙ → ∑aₖ, so (sₙ) is weakly Cauchy and a
    weak limit s would have coordinates sₖ = lim ⟨eₖ*, sₙ⟩ = 1, i.e.
    s = (1, 1, ...) ∉ c₀. Every subsequence behaves the same way, so no
    subsequence converges weakly in c₀. By Eberlein-Šmulian, c₀ is not
    reflexive (and c₀** = ℓ^∞ ∋ (1, 1, ...)). Common mistakes: confusing
    the weak topology of X* (tested against X**) with the weak* topology
    (tested against X); claiming Alaoglu gives SEQUENTIAL compactness for
    non-separable X (it fails in (ℓ^∞)*). Now you try: in ℓ^∞ = (ℓ¹)*,
    what is the weak* limit of sₙ? ((1, 1, 1, ...))

D3. Let dₙ be bounded scalars and T = diag(dₙ) on ℓ², Teₙ = dₙeₙ.
    (a) Prove T is compact ⇔ dₙ → 0, computing ‖T − Tₙ‖ where Tₙ is the
    truncation to the first n coordinates. (b) Prove that the compact
    operators form a closed subspace of B(X, Y) (Y Banach). (c) For
    dₙ = 1/n find σ(T) and classify its points.
    Answer: (a) ‖T − Tₙ‖ = sup_{k>n}|dₖ|. (b) diagonal argument / ε-nets.
    (c) σ(T) = {0} ∪ {1/n}: each 1/n an eigenvalue, 0 in σ_c.
    Teacher explains: (a) (⇐) Tₙ has finite rank, hence is compact, and
    (T − Tₙ)x = (0, ..., 0, dₙ₊₁xₙ₊₁, ...) so ‖T − Tₙ‖ = sup_{k>n}|dₖ| → 0.
    By (b) T is compact. (⇒) If dₙ ↛ 0 there are ε > 0 and n₁ < n₂ < ...
    with |d_{nⱼ}| ≥ ε. The bounded sequence e_{nⱼ} is mapped to d_{nⱼ}e_{nⱼ}
    with ‖Te_{nⱼ} − Te_{nₖ}‖ ≥ ε√2: no convergent subsequence. (b) Let
    Tₙ → T in norm, Tₙ compact, B the unit ball. Given ε, pick n with
    ‖T − Tₙ‖ < ε/3 and a finite ε/3-net {Tₙx₁, ..., Tₙx_m} of Tₙ(B)
    (totally bounded). For x ∈ B choose i with ‖Tₙx − Tₙxᵢ‖ < ε/3; then
    ‖Tx − Txᵢ‖ < ε. So T(B) is totally bounded, and relatively compact
    because Y is complete. (c) T − λ is diagonal with entries 1/k − λ; if
    λ ∉ {0} ∪ {1/k} these are bounded away from 0 and the inverse is
    bounded. Each 1/k is an eigenvalue (eigenvector eₖ) of multiplicity 1.
    At λ = 0: T is injective, its range contains every finite sequence
    (dense) but not (1/k) ∈ ℓ² (the preimage would be (1, 1, ...)), so
    0 ∈ σ_c. This matches RIESZ-SCHAUDER: nonzero spectrum = eigenvalues
    of finite multiplicity accumulating only at 0. Common mistakes: "T is
    compact because Teₙ → 0" — this is NECESSARY (eₙ ⇀ 0 and compact maps
    send weak to norm limits) but not sufficient for non-diagonal T,
    because compactness concerns the whole unit ball; forgetting that
    0 ∈ σ(T) for every compact T on an infinite-dimensional space. Now you
    try: is diag(1/log(n + 1)) compact? (yes, 1/log(n + 1) → 0)

D4. The Volterra operator on L²[0, 1] is Vf(x) = ∫₀ˣ f(y) dy.
    (a) Show V is Hilbert-Schmidt and compute ‖V‖_HS. (b) Show σ(V) = {0}
    and that V has no eigenvalues. (c) Compute ‖V‖ exactly.
    Answer: (a) ‖V‖_HS = 1/√2 ≈ 0.70711. (b) ‖Vⁿ‖ ≤ 1/(n − 1)!, so
    r(V) = 0; Vf = λf forces f = 0. (c) ‖V‖ = 2/π ≈ 0.63662.
    Teacher explains: (a) V has kernel k(x, y) = 𝟙_{y<x}, and ∬k² =
    area of the triangle = 1/2, so ‖V‖_HS = 1/√2 and V is compact.
    (b) Vⁿ has kernel (x − y)^{n−1}/(n − 1)! on y < x, so by Cauchy-Schwarz
    |Vⁿf(x)| ≤ ‖f‖₂/(n − 1)! and ‖Vⁿ‖ ≤ 1/(n − 1)!. Hence r(V) =
    lim ‖Vⁿ‖^(1/n) = 0 and σ(V) = {0} (non-empty!). If Vf = λf with λ ≠ 0
    then f = λ⁻¹Vf is continuous, then C¹, with f′ = f/λ and f(0) = 0, so
    f = 0; if Vf = 0 then differentiating gives f = 0 a.e. So σ_p = ∅; the
    range of V ⊇ C_c^∞(0, 1) is dense, so 0 ∈ σ_c. (c) ‖V‖² = ‖V*V‖, the
    largest eigenvalue of the compact positive operator V*V, where
    V*g(x) = ∫ₓ¹ g. Suppose V*Vf = μf, μ > 0, and put g = Vf, h = V*g.
    Then h(1) = 0, h′ = −g, g(0) = 0, g′ = f. From μf = h: μf(1) = 0,
    μf′ = −g so f′(0) = 0, and μf″ = −f. Thus f = cos(ωx), ω = μ^(−1/2),
    with cos ω = 0: ω = (k − ½)π and μₖ = 1/((k − ½)²π²). The largest is
    μ₁ = 4/π², so ‖V‖ = 2/π. Check: ∑μₖ = (4/π²)∑1/(2k − 1)² = (4/π²)(π²/8)
    = 1/2 = ‖V‖_HS² ✓. Common mistakes: equating ‖V‖ with ‖V‖_HS (only
    ‖V‖ ≤ ‖V‖_HS); concluding from r(V) = 0 that V = 0 (V is not normal).
    Now you try: the norm of V on L¹[0, 1]. (sup_y ∫_y¹ dx = 1)

D5. State the Fredholm alternative and use it to solve
       f(x) − λ∫₀¹ xy f(y) dy = g(x)   on L²[0, 1].
    (a) Solve for λ ≠ 3. (b) Solve for g ≡ 1, λ = 1. (c) Treat λ = 3.
    Answer: (a) f = g + λx(∫₀¹ yg)/(1 − λ/3). (b) f = 1 + 3x/4. (c) solvable
    ⇔ ∫₀¹ yg(y) dy = 0; then f = g + cx, c arbitrary.
    Teacher explains: FREDHOLM ALTERNATIVE: for K compact and μ ≠ 0,
    either (μ − K)f = g has a unique solution for every g, or (μ − K)f = 0
    has non-trivial solutions; then the solutions of the homogeneous
    adjoint equation form a space of the same finite dimension, and
    (μ − K)f = g is solvable ⇔ g ⊥ ker(μ̄ − K*). Here K = rank one:
    Kf = x⟨f, x⟩, whose only nonzero eigenvalue is ⟨x, x⟩ = 1/3 (eigen-
    function x). The equation f − λKf = g means f = g + λcx with
    c = ∫₀¹ yf(y) dy. Multiply by x and integrate: c = ∫yg + λc/3, so
    (a) c = (∫₀¹ yg)/(1 − λ/3) for λ ≠ 3. (b) ∫y·1 dy = 1/2, c = (1/2)/(2/3)
    = 3/4, f = 1 + 3x/4. Check: ∫₀¹ y(1 + 3y/4) dy = 1/2 + 1/4 = 3/4, and
    f − x·(3/4) = 1 ✓. (c) For λ = 3 the equation for c reads c = ∫yg + c,
    solvable iff ∫yg = 0 (g ⊥ x = kernel of the self-adjoint I − 3K), and
    then c is free. Example g = 1 − 3x/2: f = 1 − 3x/2 + cx. Common
    mistakes: dividing by 1 − λ/3 without checking; forgetting that
    uniqueness fails exactly when solvability becomes conditional. Now you
    try: solve f(x) − ½∫₀¹ f(y) dy = x. (f = x + 1/2)

D6. Let T be the integral operator on L²[0, 1] with kernel k(x, y) =
    min(x, y). (a) Show u = Tf solves −u″ = f, u(0) = 0, u′(1) = 0.
    (b) Find all eigenvalues and eigenfunctions and write the spectral
    decomposition of T. (c) Use Mercer's theorem to evaluate ∑1/(2k − 1)².
    Answer: (a) differentiate twice. (b) λₖ = 1/((k − ½)²π²), eₖ(x) =
    √2 sin((k − ½)πx), k ≥ 1; T = ∑λₖ⟨·, eₖ⟩eₖ, ‖T‖ = 4/π². (c) π²/8.
    Teacher explains: (a) u(x) = ∫₀ˣ yf(y) dy + x∫ₓ¹ f(y) dy. Then u′(x) =
    xf(x) + ∫ₓ¹ f − xf(x) = ∫ₓ¹ f, so u′(1) = 0, u(0) = 0, u″ = −f. So T is
    the inverse of −d²/dx² with these boundary conditions (T is its
    GREEN'S OPERATOR). (b) T is compact (continuous kernel, Hilbert-
    Schmidt) and self-adjoint (symmetric real kernel), so the SPECTRAL
    THEOREM gives an orthonormal eigenbasis with real eigenvalues → 0.
    Tu = λu with λ ≠ 0 means u = λ⁻¹Tu is smooth, u(0) = 0, u′(1) = 0 and
    −λu″ = u. So u = sin(ωx), ω = λ^(−1/2), cos ω = 0, ω = (k − ½)π. Also
    ker T = 0 (Tf = 0 ⇒ f = −(Tf)″ = 0). Normalising, ∫₀¹ 2 sin² = 1. The
    eigenvalues are 4/π², 4/(9π²), 4/(25π²), ... ≈ 0.40528, 0.04503, ...;
    ‖T‖ = λ₁ = 4/π². (c) MERCER: a continuous symmetric kernel of a
    POSITIVE operator has k(x, y) = ∑λₖeₖ(x)eₖ(y) uniformly and trace
    ∑λₖ = ∫₀¹ k(x, x) dx = ∫₀¹ x dx = 1/2. Thus (4/π²)∑1/(2k − 1)² = 1/2
    and ∑1/(2k − 1)² = π²/8, whence ζ(2) = (4/3)(π²/8) = π²/6. Also
    ‖T‖_HS² = ∬min(x, y)² = 1/6 = ∑λₖ², giving ∑1/(2k − 1)⁴ = π⁴/96.
    Common mistakes: forgetting to check that 0 is not an eigenvalue
    before claiming the eigenvectors are a basis of all of L²; using
    Mercer for kernels that are not positive. Now you try: the kernel
    min(x, y) − xy (Dirichlet Green's function): eigenvalues and trace?
    (1/(k²π²), k ≥ 1; trace ∫(x − x²) = 1/6, so ζ(2) = π²/6)

D7. Let S be the right shift on ℓ²(N), S(x₁, x₂, ...) = (0, x₁, x₂, ...),
    and L = S* the left shift. Find σ(S) and σ(L) and split each into
    point, continuous and residual spectrum.
    Answer: σ(S) = σ(L) = D̄ (closed unit disc). S: σ_p = ∅, σ_r = D (open
    disc), σ_c = T (unit circle). L: σ_p = D, σ_c = T, σ_r = ∅.
    Teacher explains: ‖S‖ = ‖L‖ = 1, so both spectra lie in D̄.
    EIGENVALUES OF L: Lx = λx means x_{n+1} = λxₙ, so x = x₁(1, λ, λ², ...),
    which lies in ℓ² iff |λ| < 1. So σ_p(L) = D. EIGENVALUES OF S: Sx = λx
    gives 0 = λx₁ and xₙ = λx_{n+1}; for λ = 0, Sx = 0 ⇒ x = 0; for λ ≠ 0,
    x₁ = 0 and then all xₙ = 0. So σ_p(S) = ∅. RESIDUAL PART OF S: for
    |λ| < 1 put v = (1, λ̄, λ̄², ...) ∈ ℓ², so (L − λ̄)v = 0 and
    ⟨(S − λ)x, v⟩ = ⟨x, (L − λ̄)v⟩ = 0 for all x: the range of S − λ is
    orthogonal to v ≠ 0, not dense. As S − λ is injective, λ ∈ σ_r(S).
    THE CIRCLE: the spectrum is closed, so T ⊆ σ(S). For |λ| = 1, S − λ is
    injective, and its range is dense because (L − λ̄)v = 0 has no ℓ²
    solution (|λ̄| = 1); so λ ∈ σ_c(S). For L: if |λ| < 1, λ ∈ σ_p(L); for
    |λ| = 1, L − λ is injective and its range is dense since S − λ̄ is
    injective (ran(L − λ)^⊥ = ker(S − λ̄) = 0): continuous spectrum. Here
    σ_r(L) = ∅ matches the rule λ ∈ σ_r(T) ⇒ λ̄ ∈ σ_p(T*). Common mistakes:
    believing an operator's spectrum is its set of eigenvalues (S has
    none!); forgetting the conjugate λ̄ when passing to the adjoint. Now you
    try: the spectrum of M_x on L²[0, 2] and its type. ([0, 2], all
    continuous spectrum, no eigenvalues)

D8. State the continuous functional calculus for a self-adjoint operator.
    For A = [[2, 1], [1, 2]] compute the spectral projections, √A and e^A.
    Prove that for normal T, ‖(T − λ)⁻¹‖ = 1/dist(λ, σ(T)), and evaluate it
    for T = M_x on L²[0, 1] at λ = 2 and at λ = ½ + i/2.
    Answer: P₃ = ½[[1, 1], [1, 1]], P₁ = ½[[1, −1], [−1, 1]];
    √A = ½[[√3 + 1, √3 − 1], [√3 − 1, √3 + 1]] ≈ [[1.36603, 0.36603],
    [0.36603, 1.36603]]; e^A = ½[[e³ + e, e³ − e], [e³ − e, e³ + e]];
    resolvent norms 1 and 2.
    Teacher explains: For T normal (e.g. self-adjoint) there is a unique
    isometric *-homomorphism C(σ(T)) → B(H), f ↦ f(T), with 1 ↦ I and
    t ↦ T; it satisfies ‖f(T)‖ = sup_{σ(T)}|f| and σ(f(T)) = f(σ(T)). For
    a matrix, f(A) = ∑f(λ)P_λ over the eigenvalues. Here det(A − λ) =
    (2 − λ)² − 1, so σ(A) = {1, 3}, eigenvectors (1, 1)/√2 for 3 and
    (1, −1)/√2 for 1; P₃, P₁ as above, A = 3P₃ + P₁. Hence
    √A = √3P₃ + P₁ and e^A = e³P₃ + eP₁, as stated; check (√A)² =
    3P₃ + P₁ = A since P₃P₁ = 0. For the resolvent, f(t) = 1/(t − λ) is
    continuous on σ(T) when λ ∉ σ(T), and f(T)(T − λ) = I, so
    ‖(T − λ)⁻¹‖ = sup_{t∈σ(T)} 1/|t − λ| = 1/dist(λ, σ(T)). For M_x,
    σ = [0, 1]: dist(2, [0, 1]) = 1 gives 1; dist(½ + i/2, [0, 1]) = ½
    gives 2. For NON-normal operators only ≥ holds (the Volterra operator
    has σ = {0}, yet ‖(V − λ)⁻¹‖ grows like e^(1/λ)/λ, not like 1/λ, as
    λ ↓ 0). Common mistakes: taking square roots entrywise (√2 on the
    diagonal is WRONG); forgetting that √ needs A ≥ 0 (σ ⊆ [0, ∞)). Now
    you try: √[[5, 4], [4, 5]]. ([[2, 1], [1, 2]])

D9. Let A be a complex unital Banach algebra. (a) Prove that σ(a) is
    non-empty and deduce the Gelfand-Mazur theorem. (b) Verify the
    spectral radius formula for a = [[1, 1], [0, 1]] ∈ M₂(C) with the
    operator 2-norm. (c) Show that the spectrum of z is the closed disc in
    the disc algebra A(D) but the circle in C(T).
    Answer: (a) Liouville applied to λ ↦ f((a − λ)⁻¹), f ∈ A*.
    (b) ‖aⁿ‖^(1/n) → 1 = r(a), although ‖a‖ = (1 + √5)/2 ≈ 1.61803.
    (c) 1/(z − λ) ∈ A(D) ⇔ |λ| > 1; in C(T) ⇔ |λ| ≠ 1.
    Teacher explains: (a) G(A) is open: if ‖1 − b‖ < 1 then b = 1 −
    (1 − b) is invertible by the Neumann series; the resolvent R(λ) =
    (a − λ)⁻¹ is analytic on the open set C ∖ σ(a), and for |λ| > ‖a‖,
    ‖R(λ)‖ ≤ 1/(|λ| − ‖a‖) → 0. If σ(a) = ∅, then for each f ∈ A* the
    function λ ↦ f(R(λ)) is entire and tends to 0, hence ≡ 0 (Liouville);
    by Hahn-Banach R(λ) = 0, impossible for an invertible element. So
    σ(a) ≠ ∅. GELFAND-MAZUR: if every non-zero element is invertible, pick
    λ ∈ σ(a); a − λ is not invertible, so a − λ = 0, a = λ·1: A = C.
    (Over R this fails: the quaternions H.) (b) aⁿ = [[1, n], [0, 1]], so
    ‖aⁿ‖ ≥ n and ‖aⁿ‖ ≤ n + 1 (bounded by 1 + n); hence ‖aⁿ‖^(1/n) → 1 =
    r(a) = max|eigenvalue|. ‖a‖² = largest eigenvalue of aᵀa =
    [[1, 1], [1, 2]], i.e. (3 + √5)/2, so ‖a‖ = (1 + √5)/2: r(a) < ‖a‖
    for this non-normal element. (c) z − λ is invertible in A(D) iff
    1/(z − λ) is holomorphic in D and continuous on D̄ iff |λ| > 1; so σ =
    D̄. In C(T) we only need z − λ ≠ 0 on |z| = 1: σ = T. In general if
    A ⊂ B then σ_B(a) ⊆ σ_A(a) and ∂σ_A(a) ⊆ σ_B(a): passing to a
    subalgebra can only "fill in holes". Common mistakes: writing r(a) =
    ‖a‖ for all a; assuming spectrum is intrinsic to the element (it is,
    for C*-subalgebras). Now you try: r of a = [[0, 2], [8, 0]]. (4:
    a² = 16I, eigenvalues ±4)

D10. (a) Determine the characters of ℓ¹(Z) (convolution) and its Gelfand
     transform. (b) State Wiener's lemma. (c) Show a = 5δ₀ + 2δ₁ + 2δ₋₁ is
     invertible in ℓ¹(Z), find σ(a), r(a) and a⁻¹. (d) With the involution
     (a*)ₙ = conj(a₋ₙ), show ℓ¹(Z) is not a C*-algebra.
     Answer: (a) φ_z(a) = ∑aₙzⁿ, |z| = 1; â is a Fourier series.
     (b) non-vanishing absolutely convergent Fourier series ⇒ 1/f also.
     (c) σ(a) = [1, 9], r(a) = 9 = ‖a‖, (a⁻¹)ₙ = (1/3)(−1/2)^|n|.
     (d) a = δ₀ + δ₁ − δ₂: ‖a*a‖₁ = 5 ≠ 9 = ‖a‖₁².
     Teacher explains: (a) A character φ is determined by z = φ(δ₁), since
     δₙ = δ₁^{*n}. Characters have norm ≤ 1, so |z| ≤ ‖δ₁‖ = 1 and
     |1/z| = |φ(δ₋₁)| ≤ 1: |z| = 1. Conversely each z ∈ T gives φ_z(a) =
     ∑aₙzⁿ (multiplicative because it turns convolution into products).
     So Δ = T and â(e^{iθ}) = ∑aₙe^{inθ}. (b) By Gelfand theory, a is
     invertible ⇔ â never vanishes on Δ. If f(θ) = ∑aₙe^{inθ} with
     ∑|aₙ| < ∞ has no zero, then a⁻¹ ∈ ℓ¹(Z) and 1/f = ∑(a⁻¹)ₙe^{inθ}:
     WIENER'S LEMMA, proved without any estimate! (c) â(θ) = 5 + 4cos θ ∈
     [1, 9], never 0: invertible, σ(a) = â(T) = [1, 9], r(a) = ‖â‖_∞ = 9 =
     ‖a‖₁. For the inverse, 5 + 2z + 2/z = (2z + 1)(z + 2)/z, and
        z/((2z + 1)(z + 2)) = (2/3)/(z + 2) − (1/3)/(2z + 1).
     On |z| = 1 expand (2/3)/(z + 2) = (1/3)∑_{m≥0}(−z/2)ᵐ and
     −(1/3)/(2z + 1) = −(1/(6z))∑_{k≥0}(−1/(2z))ᵏ = (1/3)∑_{k≥1}(−1/2)ᵏz⁻ᵏ.
     So bₙ = (1/3)(−1/2)^|n|. Check n = 0: 5/3 + 2(−1/6) + 2(−1/6) = 1 ✓;
     n = 1: 5(−1/6) + 2(1/3) + 2(1/12) = 0 ✓. ‖a⁻¹‖₁ = (1/3)(1 + 2) = 1.
     (d) a*a has entries (a*a)ₙ = ∑ₖ aₖaₖ₊ₙ: 3 at n = 0, 0 at n = ±1, −1 at
     n = ±2, so ‖a*a‖ = 5 while ‖a‖² = 9. By contrast the Gelfand transform
     maps ℓ¹(Z) into the C*-algebra C(T) (GELFAND-NAIMARK: every commutative
     unital C*-algebra IS some C(Δ)), injectively but not isometrically.
     Common mistakes: using the unit disc (that is ℓ¹(N₀), where only
     n ≥ 0 occur); expanding 1/(2z + 1) in powers of z on |z| = 1 (that
     series diverges there). Now you try: invert δ₀ − ½δ₁ in ℓ¹(Z).
     (∑_{n≥0} 2⁻ⁿδₙ)

D11. Working in D′(R): (a) compute the first two distributional
     derivatives of |x| and of e^(−|x|); (b) prove (log|x|)′ = p.v. 1/x;
     (c) show xδ′ = −δ and x·p.v.(1/x) = 1; (d) show δ is not a regular
     distribution; (e) using the Fourier transform, find a fundamental
     solution of −u″ + u and solve −u″ + u = f.
     Answer: (a) |x|′ = sign x, |x|″ = 2δ; (e^(−|x|))″ = e^(−|x|) − 2δ.
     (b) integrate by parts on |x| > ε. (c) test against φ. (d) a
     shrinking-support argument. (e) E = ½e^(−|x|), u = E * f.
     Teacher explains: (a) ⟨|x|′, φ⟩ = −∫|x|φ′ = −∫₀^∞ xφ′ + ∫_{−∞}^0 xφ′
     = ∫₀^∞ φ − ∫_{−∞}^0 φ = ⟨sign, φ⟩ (boundary terms vanish because
     x = 0 at 0). Then ⟨sign′, φ⟩ = −∫₀^∞ φ′ + ∫_{−∞}^0 φ′ = 2φ(0): |x|″ =
     2δ. RULE: a jump of size J at a in f produces J δ_a in f′. For
     e^(−|x|): derivative −sign(x)e^(−|x|) (continuous function has no
     jump), which jumps by −2 at 0, so (e^(−|x|))″ = e^(−|x|) − 2δ.
     (b) −∫_{|x|>ε} log|x|φ′ = log ε(φ(ε) − φ(−ε)) + ∫_{|x|>ε} φ(x)/x dx
     by parts; φ(ε) − φ(−ε) = O(ε) and ε log ε → 0, so letting ε → 0,
     ⟨(log|x|)′, φ⟩ = lim ∫_{|x|>ε} φ/x = ⟨p.v. 1/x, φ⟩. (c) ⟨xδ′, φ⟩ =
     ⟨δ′, xφ⟩ = −(xφ)′(0) = −φ(0); ⟨x·p.v.1/x, φ⟩ = lim∫_{|x|>ε} xφ/x = ∫φ.
     (d) If ∫fφ = φ(0) for all φ with f ∈ L¹_loc, take φₙ(x) = φ(nx) with
     φ(0) = 1, 0 ≤ φ ≤ 1, supp φ ⊆ [−1, 1]: 1 = ∫fφₙ ≤ ∫_{|x|≤1/n}|f| → 0 by
     dominated convergence, contradiction. (e) With f̂(ξ) = ∫f e^(−ixξ),
     (−u″ + u)^ = (1 + ξ²)û, so Ê = 1/(1 + ξ²). Since (e^(−|x|))^ =
     ∫e^(−|x|−ixξ) dx = 1/(1 + iξ) + 1/(1 − iξ) = 2/(1 + ξ²), E = ½e^(−|x|);
     by (a), −E″ + E = −½(e^(−|x|) − 2δ) + ½e^(−|x|) = δ ✓. Then
     u(x) = ½∫e^(−|x−y|)f(y) dy. Common mistakes: forgetting the δ terms
     from jumps; writing δ(x) = "∞ at 0" and multiplying two δ's (δ² is
     undefined). Now you try: the derivative of ⌊x⌋. (∑_{n∈Z} δₙ)

D12. (a) Let u(x) = |x|^α on the unit ball B ⊂ Rⁿ. Show u ∈ W^{1,p}(B)
     iff α > 1 − n/p (α ≠ 0) and deduce that H¹(B) ⊄ L^∞ when n ≥ 2; give
     a bounded-domain example in R² with only a log log singularity.
     (b) Prove H^s(R) ⊂ C₀(R) for s > 1/2 with ‖u‖_∞ ≤ C_s‖u‖_{H^s}.
     (c) Prove the Poincaré inequality ‖u‖₂ ≤ (1/π)‖u′‖₂ on H¹₀(0, 1),
     with equality for sin πx, and state Rellich's theorem.
     Answer: (a) |∇u| = |α||x|^{α−1} ∈ Lᵖ ⇔ (α − 1)p + n > 0; n = 3,
     p = 2: |x|^(−1/4) ∈ H¹ unbounded; log log(e/|x|) ∈ H¹(B ⊂ R²).
     (b) |u(x)| ≤ (1/2π)∫|û| and Cauchy-Schwarz. (c) Fourier sine series.
     Teacher explains: (a) Away from 0, ∇u = α|x|^{α−2}x, |∇u| = |α|r^{α−1}.
     In polar coordinates ∫_B r^{βp} dx = c∫₀¹ r^{βp+n−1} dr < ∞ ⇔ βp + n >
     0. So u ∈ Lᵖ ⇔ αp + n > 0 and |∇u| ∈ Lᵖ ⇔ (α − 1)p + n > 0, which
     implies the former. The pointwise gradient is the WEAK gradient: for
     φ ∈ C_c^∞(B), integrate by parts on B ∖ B_ε; the boundary term is
     ≤ Cε^α·ε^{n−1}‖φ‖_∞ → 0 since α + n − 1 > 0 (as α > 1 − n/p ≥
     1 − n). For n = 3, p = 2 the condition is α > −1/2, so |x|^(−1/4) is
     an unbounded H¹ function. For n = 2, p = 2 no power works (α > 0), but
     u = log log(e/|x|) has |∇u| = 1/(r log(e/r)) and ∫_B|∇u|² =
     2π∫₀¹ dr/(r log²(e/r)) = 2π∫₁^∞ dt/t² = 2π (t = log(e/r)): u ∈ H¹, yet
     u → ∞ at 0. (b) For u ∈ S, u(x) = (1/2π)∫û(ξ)e^{ixξ} dξ, so
        |u(x)| ≤ (1/2π)(∫(1 + ξ²)^(−s) dξ)^(1/2)(∫(1 + ξ²)^s|û|²)^(1/2),
     and ∫(1 + ξ²)^(−s) < ∞ iff 2s > 1. Since û ∈ L¹, u is continuous and
     → 0 at ∞ (Riemann-Lebesgue); extend by density. In Rⁿ the condition
     is s > n/2. (c) Write u = ∑bₖ√2 sin(kπx) (complete in L²(0, 1)); for
